Generalized Low-rank plus Sparse Tensor Estimation by Fast Riemannian Optimization
Jian-Feng Cai, Jingyang Li, Dong Xia

Abstract
We investigate a generalized framework to estimate a latent low-rank plus sparse tensor, where the low-rank tensor often captures the multi-way principal components and the sparse tensor accounts for potential model mis-specifications or heterogeneous signals that are unexplainable by the low-rank part. The framework flexibly covers both linear and generalized linear models, and can easily handle continuous or categorical variables. We propose a fast algorithm by integrating the Riemannian gradient descent and a novel gradient pruning procedure. Under suitable conditions, the algorithm converges linearly and can simultaneously estimate both the low-rank and sparse tensors. The statistical error bounds of final estimates are established in terms of the gradient of loss function. The error bounds are generally sharp under specific statistical models, e.g., the sub-Gaussian robust PCA and Bernoulli tensor model. Moreover, our method achieves non-trivial error bounds for heavy-tailed tensor PCA whenever the noise has a finite 2 + ε 2+\varepsilon moment. We apply our method to analyze the international trade flow dataset and the statistician hypergraph co-authorship network, both yielding new and interesting findings.



1
Introduction

In recent years, massive
multi-way
datasets, often called
tensor
data, have routinely arisen in diverse fields. An
m
th-order tensor is a multilinear array with
m
ways, e.g., matrices are second order tensors. These multi-way structures often emerge when, to name a few, information features are collected from distinct domains
(
Bi et al. 2020
;
Liu et al. 2017
;
Han et al. 2020
;
Bi et al. 2018
;
Zhang et al. 2020b
;
Wang and Zeng 2019
)
, the multi-relational interactions or higher-order interactions of entities are present
(
Ke et al. 2019
;
Jing et al. 2020
;
Luo and Zhang 2020
;
Wang and Li 2020
;
Pensky and Zhang 2019
)
, or the higher-order moments of data are explored
(
Anandkumar et al. 2014
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Hao et al. 2020
)
. There is an increasing demand for effective methods to analyze large and complex tensorial datasets.
Low-rank tensor models are a class of statistical models for describing and analyzing tensor datasets. At its core is the assumption that the observed data obeys a distribution that is characterized by a
latent
low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
. Oftentimes, analyzing tensor datasets boils down to estimating the low-rank
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
. This procedure is usually referred to as the
low-rank tensor estimation
. Together with specifically designed algorithms, low-rank tensor methods have demonstrated encouraging performances on many real-world applications and datasets such as the spatial and temporal pattern analysis of human brain developments
(
Liu et al. 2017
)
, community detection on multi-layer networks and hypergraph networks
(
Jing et al. 2020
;
Ke et al. 2019
;
Wang and Li 2020
)
, multi-dimensional recommender system
(
Bi et al. 2018
)
, learning the hidden components of mixture models
(
Anandkumar et al. 2014
)
, analysis of brain dynamic functional connectivity
(
Sun and Li 2019
)
, image denoising and recovery
(
Xia et al. 2021
)
and etc.

However, the exact low-rank assumption is stringent and sometimes untrue, making low-rank tensor methods vulnerable under model misspecification or in the existence of outliers or heterogeneous signals. While low-rank structure underscores the multi-way principal components, it fails to capture the dimension-specific outliers or heterogeneous signals that often carry distinctive and useful information. Consider the international trade flow dataset (see Section
8
) that forms a third-order tensor by the dimensions
{\rm countries}\times{\rm countries}\times{\rm commodities}
. On the one hand we observe that the low-rank structure is capable to reflect the shared similarities among countries such as their geographical locations and economic structures, but on the other hand the low-rank structure tends to disregard the heterogeneity in the trading flows of different countries. This vital heterogeneity often reveals distinctive trading patterns of certain commodities for some countries. Moreover, the heterogeneous signals are usually full-rank and strong that can deteriorate the estimates of the multi-way low-rank principal components. We indeed observe that by filtering out these outliers or heterogeneous signals, the resultant low-rank estimates become more insightful. It is therefore advantageous to decouple the low-rank signal and the heterogeneous one in the procedure of low-rank tensor estimation. Fortunately, these outliers or heterogeneous signals are usually representable by a
sparse
tensor, which, is identifiable in generalized low-rank tensor models under suitable conditions.

In this paper, we propose a generalized low-rank plus sparse tensor model to analyze tensorial datasets. Our fundamental assumption is that the observed data is sampled from a statistical model characterized by the latent tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. We assume
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
to be low-rank capturing the multi-way principal components, and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
to be sparse (the precise definition of being “sparse” can be found in Section
2
) addressing potential model mis-specifications, outliers or heterogeneous signals that are unexplainable by the low-rank part. Our framework is very flexible which covers both linear and generalized linear models, and can easily handle both quantitative and categorical data. Compared with existing literature on low-rank tensor methods
(
Gu et al. 2014
;
Xia et al. 2021
;
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Yuan and Zhang 2017
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Hao et al. 2020
;
Xia 2019
)
, our framework and method are more robust, particularly when the latent tensor is only
approximately
low-rank or when the noise have heavy tails. A special case of our model, robust sub-Gaussian tensor PCA, was proposed in
Gu et al. 2014
. Their method was based on matrix unfolding and is thus statistically sub-optimal. The robust tensor PCA model studied by
Zhou and Feng 2017
was based on low tubal-rank and their method is sub-optimal for treating low Tucker-rank tensors.
The Bernoulli tensor model introduced in
Wang and Li 2020
;
Yu and Liu 2016
, which cannot handle sparse corruptions, is also a special case of our model. See Table
1
for the comparison with related works. Compared with the aforementioned works on robust tensor estimation, our model is more general covering a much wider spectrum of tensor-related applications and our method deals with nearly all kinds of tensor data – Poisson (if intensity is strong), Bernoulli, heavy-tailed data, to name but a few. For instance, to our best knowledge, we derive the first non-trivial convergence rate for heavy-tailed tensor PCA. Meanwhile, our method is robust to model mis-specification up to sparse corruptions. See numerical comparison results in Section
7
. We note that the generalized low-rank plus sparse matrix model has been investigated by
Zhang et al. 2018
and
Robin et al. 2020
. However, the estimating procedure is more involved for tensors, the technical proofs are more challenging, and treating tensors by matrix unfolding is generally statistically sub-optimal.

With a properly chosen loss function
\mathfrak{L}(\cdot)
, our estimating procedure is formulated into an optimization framework, which aims at minimizing
\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}})
subject to the low-rank and sparse constraints on
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
, respectively. We propose a new and fast algorithm to solve for the underlying tensors of interest. The algorithm is iterative and consists of two main ingredients: the
Riemannian gradient descent
and the
gradient pruning
. By viewing the low-rank solution as a point on the Riemannian manifold, we adopt Riemannian gradient descent to update the low-rank estimate. Basically, the Riemannian gradient is the projection of the
vanilla gradient

\nabla\mathfrak{L}
onto
the tangent space of a Riemannian manifold. Unlike the vanilla gradient that is usually full-rank, the Riemannian gradient is often low-rank which can significantly boost up the speed of updating the low-rank estimate. Provided with a reliable estimate of the low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
, the gradient pruning is a fast procedure to update our estimate of the sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. It is based on the belief that, under suitable conditions, if the current estimate
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
is close to
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
entry-wisely, the entries of the gradient
\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
should have small magnitudes on the complement of the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. Then it suffices to run a screening of the entries of
\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
, locate its entries with large magnitudes and choose
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
to minimize the magnitudes of those entries of
\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}})
. The procedure looks like pruning the gradient
\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
– thus the name gradient pruning. The algorithm alternates between Riemannian gradient descent and gradient pruning until reaching a locally optimal solution.

1.1
Our Contributions

We propose a novel and generalized framework to analyze tensor datasets. Our framework allows the latent tensor to be high-rank, as long as it is within the sparse perturbations of a low-rank tensor. The sparse tensor can account for potential mis-specifications of the exact low-rank tensor models, making our framework robust to outliers, heavy-tailed distributions, heterogeneous signals, etc. Meanwhile, our framework flexibly covers both linear and generalized linear models, and is applicable to both continuous and categorical variables.
Details are summarize in Table
1
.

Table 1:
Comparison with related literature. Here, GLM stands for generalized linear model. For the sub-Gaussian robust PCA (SG-RPCA) error rate, we assume
d_{j}\asymp d,j\in[m]
for simplicity. We remark that a strong Poisson intensity is required by RGrad. See Lemma 8.2 in the supplement.

We develop a new and fast algorithm which can simultaneously estimate both the low-rank and the sparse tensors. The algorithm is based on the integration of Riemannian gradient descent and a novel gradient pruning procedure. Our proposed method works for both linear and generalized linear models, adapts to additional sparse perturbations, and is reliable in the existence of stochastic noise. We prove, in a general framework, that our algorithm converges fast even with fixed step-sizes, and establish the statistical error bounds of final estimates. The error bounds are sharp and proportional to the intrinsic degrees of freedom under many specific statistical models.

To showcase the superiority of our methods, we consider applying our framework to interesting examples. The first application is on the sub-Gaussian robust tensor principal component analysis (SG-RPCA) where the observation is simply
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
with additive sub-Gaussian noise. We show that our method can recover both
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
with sharp error bounds, and recover the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
under fairly weak conditions.
The second example is on the tensor PCA when the noise has heavy tails. We show that our framework is naturally immune to the potential outliers caused by the heavy-tailed noise, and demonstrate that our method achieves non-trivial error bounds as long as the noise have a finite
2+\varepsilon
moment. This bridges a fundamental gap in the understanding of tensor PCA since the existing methods are usually effective only under sub-Gaussian or sub-Exponential noise. We then apply our framework to learn the latent low-rank structure
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
from a binary tensorial observation, assuming the Bernoulli tensor model with a general link function, e.g., the logistic and probit link. Compared with the existing literature, our method is robust and allows an arbitrary but sparse corruption. Finally, our method is applied to Poisson tensor RPCA under a strong intensity condition. To our best knowledge, our results are the first in these three applications. We also provide computationally fast methods to obtain good initializations.

Lastly, we employ our method to analyze two real-world datasets: the international commodity trade flow network (continuous variables) and the statistician hypergraph co-authorship network (binary variables). We observe that the low-rank plus sparse tensor framework yields intriguing and new findings that are unseen by the exact low-rank tensor methods. The sparse tensor can nicely capture informative patterns which are overlooked by the multi-way principal components.

1.2
Notations and Preliminaries of Tensor

We use calligraphic-font bold-face letters (e.g.
\bm{\mathcal{T}},\bm{\mathcal{X}},\bm{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
) to denote tensors, bold-face capital letters (e.g.
\mathbf{T},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{T}_{1}
) for matrices, bold-face lower-case letters (e.g.
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{t}_{1}
) for vectors and blackboard bold-faced letters (e.g.
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{M},\mathbb{U},\mathbb{T}
) for sets. We use square brackets with subscripts (e,g.
[\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}},[\mathbf{T}]_{i_{1},i_{2}},[\mathbf{t}]_{i_{1}}
) to represent corresponding entries of tensors, matrices and vectors, respectively. Denote
[\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{i_{1},:,:}
and
[\mathbf{T}]_{i_{1},:}
the
i_{1}
-th frontal-face and
i_{1}
-th row-vector of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathbf{T}
, respectively.
Denote
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
the Frobenius norm of matrices and tensors, and denote
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{p}}
the
\ell_{p}
-norm of vectors or vectorized tensors for
0\leq p\leq\infty
. Thus,
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\ell_{0}}
represents the number of non-zero entries of
\mathbf{v}
, and
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
denotes the largest magnitude of the entries of
\mathbf{v}
. The
j
-th canonical basis vector is written as
\mathbf{e}_{j}
whose actual dimension might vary at different appearances. We denote
C,C_{1},C_{2},c,c_{1},c_{2}\cdots
some absolute constants whose actual values can change at different lines.

An
m
-th order tensor is an
m
-way array, e.g.,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
means that its
j
-th dimension has size
d_{j}
. Thus,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
has in total
d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
entries.
The
j
-th matricization (also called unfolding)
{\cal M}_{j}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}\times d_{j}^{-}}
with
d_{j}^{-}=(d_{1}\cdots d_{m})/d_{j}
is a linear mapping so that, for example if
m=3
,
[{\cal M}_{1}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})\big]_{i_{1},(i_{2}-1)d_{3}+i_{3}}=[\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}
for
\forall i_{j}\in[d_{j}]
. Then, the collection
{\rm rank}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}):=\big({\rm rank}({\cal M}_{1}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})),\cdots,{\rm rank}({\cal M}_{m}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}))\big)^{\top}
is called the multi-linear ranks or
Tucker ranks
of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
. Given a matrix
\mathbf{W}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{j}\times d_{j}}
for any
j\in[m]
, the multi-linear product, denoted by
\times_{j}
, between
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathbf{W}_{j}
is defined by

[\bm{\mathcal{T}}\times_{j}\mathbf{W}_{j}]_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}}:=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{d_{j}}[\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j-1},k,i_{j+1},\cdots,i_{m}}\cdot[\mathbf{W}_{j}]_{i_{j},k},\ \forall i_{j^{\prime}}\in[d_{j^{\prime}}]\textrm{ for }j^{\prime}\neq j;\forall i_{j}\in[p_{j}].

If
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
has Tucker ranks
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{m})^{\top}
, there exist
\bm{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{m}}
and
\mathbf{U}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}\times r_{j}}
satisfying
\mathbf{U}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{j}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{j}}
for all
j\in[m]
such that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbf{C}\cdot\llbracket\mathbf{U}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{U}_{m}\rrbracket:=\bm{\mathcal{C}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{m}\mathbf{U}_{m}.

This is referred to as the Tucker decomposition of a low-rank tensor. Tucker ranks and decomposition are well-defined. Readers are suggested to refer
(
Kolda and Bader 2009
)
for more details and examples on tensor decomposition and tensor algebra.

2
General Low-rank plus Sparse Tensor Model

Suppose that we observe data
\mathfrak{D}
, which can be, for instance, simply a tensorial observation such as the binary adjacency tensor of a hypergraph network or multi-layer network
(
Ke et al. 2019
;
Jing et al. 2020
;
Luo and Zhang 2020
;
Wang and Li 2020
;
Jin 2015
;
Ji and Jin 2016
)
; a real-valued tensor describing multi-dimensional observations
(
Han et al. 2020
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Sun and Li 2019
;
Liu et al. 2017
)
; or a collection of pairs of tensor covariate and real-valued response
(
Hao et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020a
;
Xia et al. 2020
;
Raskutti et al. 2019
;
Chen et al. 2019
)
.
At the core of our model is the assumption that the observed
\mathfrak{D}
is sampled from a distribution characterized by a latent large tensor, denoted by
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
, where
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
has small multi-linear ranks and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
is sparse. Unlike the exact low-rank tensor models, the additional sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
can account for potential model mis-specifications and outliers. Consider that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
has multi-linear ranks
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{m})^{\top}
with
r_{j}\ll d_{j}
so that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
where

\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}:=\big\{\bm{\mathcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}:{\rm rank}\big({\cal M}_{j}(\bm{\mathcal{W}})\big)\leq r_{j},\ \forall j\in[m]\big\}.

As for the sparse tensor, we assume that each slice of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
has at most
\alpha
-portion of entries being non-zero for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We write
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}
where the latter is defined by

\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}:=\big\{\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}:\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}{\cal M}_{j}(\bm{\mathcal{S}})\|_{\ell_{0}}\leq\alpha d_{j}^{-},\ \forall j\in[m],i\in[d_{j}]\big\},

where
\mathbf{e}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th canonical basis vector whose dimension varies at different appearances.

When the low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
is also sparse, it is generally impossible to distinguish between
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
and its sparse counterpart
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. To make
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
identifiable, we assume that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
satisfies the
spikiness condition
meaning that the information it carries spreads fairly across nearly all its entries. Put differently, the spikiness condition enforces
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
to be dense – thus distinguishable from the sparse
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
.
This is a typical condition in robust matrix estimation
(
Candès et al. 2011
;
Chen et al. 2020
)
and tensor completion
(
Xia and Yuan 2019
;
Xia et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2019
)
. For exact low-rank tensor models where
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
is absent, this assumption is generally not required. See Section
6
in the supplementary file for more details.

Assumption 1
.

Let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\star}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
, and suppose there exists
\mu_{1}>0
such that the following holds:

\textsf{Spiki}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}):=(d^{*})^{1/2}\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}/\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\mu_{1},

where
d^{\ast}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
.

We denote
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}}:=\big\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}:\textsf{Spiki}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})\leq\mu_{1}\big\}
the set of low-rank tensors with spikiness bounded by
\mu_{1}
.

Relation between spikiness condition and incoherence condition.

Let
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
admit a Tucker decomposition
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}=\bm{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}\cdot\llbracket\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\ast},\cdots,\mathbf{U}_{m}^{\ast}\rrbracket
with
\bm{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{m}}
and
\mathbf{U}_{j}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}\times r_{j}}
satisfying
\mathbf{U}_{j}^{\ast\top}\mathbf{U}_{j}^{\ast}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{j}}
for all
j\in[m]
. Suppose that there exists
\mu_{0}>0
so that

\mu(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}):=\max_{j\in[m]}\ \max_{i\in[d_{j}]}\ \|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{j}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{2}}\cdot(d_{j}/r_{j})^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\mu_{0}}.

Then,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
is said to satisfy the incoherence condition with constant
\mu_{0}
.
The spikiness condition implies the incoherence condition
and vice versa
. See Lemma
13.5
.

After observing data
\mathfrak{D}
, our goal is to estimate the underlying
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast},\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\in(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha})
. Oftentimes, the problem is formulated as an optimization program equipped with a properly chosen loss function. More specifically, let
\mathfrak{L}(\cdot):=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a smooth (see Assumption
2
) loss function whose actual form depends on the particular applications. The estimators of
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast},\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})
are then defined by

(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\gamma},\ \widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\gamma}):=\argmin_{\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}}\ \mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}),

where
\gamma>1
is a tuning parameter determining the desired sparsity level of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\gamma}
. For ease of exposition, we tentatively assume that the true ranks are known. In real-world applications,
\mathbf{r},\alpha
can be selected by a BIC-type criterion (
3.3
). See Section
7
for more details.
This generalized framework covers many interesting and important examples as special cases. These examples are investigated more closely in Section
5
.

Example 2.1
.

(Tensor robust principal component analysis)
For tensor RPCA, the data observed is simply a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
. The basic assumption of tensor PCA is the existence of a low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
, called the “signal”, planted inside of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
. See, e.g.
(
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Richard and Montanari 2014
)
and references therein. The exact low-rank condition on the “signal” is sometimes stringent. Tensor robust PCA
(
Lu et al. 2016
;
Robin et al. 2020
)
relaxes this condition by assuming that the “signal” is the sum of a low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
and a sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. With additional additive stochastic noise, the Sub-Gaussian RPCA (SG-RPCA) model assumes
\bm{\mathcal{A}}=\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
with
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast},\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\in(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha})
and
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
being a noise tensor having i.i.d. random centered sub-Gaussian entries. We reserve RPCA exclusively for SG-RPCA in the subsequent chapters.
Given
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
, the goal is to estimate
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. A suitable loss function is

\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}):=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}-\bm{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
, which measures the goodness-of-fit by
\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}
to data. The estimator
(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\gamma},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\gamma})
is thus defined by

\displaystyle(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\gamma},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\gamma}):=\argmin_{\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}-\bm{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\rm F}^{2}.

(2.1)

Example 2.2
.

(Learning low-rank structure from binary tensor)
In many applications, the observed data
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
is merely a binary tensor. Examples include the adjacency tensor in multi-layer networks
(
Jing et al. 2020
;
Paul and Chen 2020
)
, brain structural connectivity networks
(
Wang et al. 2019
;
Wang and Li 2020
)
and etc. Following the Bernoulli tensor model proposed in
(
Wang and Li 2020
)
or generalizing the
1
-bit matrix completion model
(
Davenport et al. 2014
)
, we assume that there exist
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast},\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\in(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha})
satisfying

[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Bernoulli}\big(p([\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega})\big),\forall\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}],

where
p(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\mapsto[0,1]
is a suitable inverse link function. Popular choices of
p(\cdot)
include the logistic link
p(x)=(1+e^{-x/\sigma})^{-1}
and probit link
p(x)=1-\Phi(-x/\sigma)
where
\sigma>0
is a scaling parameter. We note that, due to potential symmetry in networks, the entry independence statement might only hold for a subset of its entries (e.g., upper-triangular entries in a single-layer undirected network). Compared with the exact low-rank Bernoulli tensor model
(
Wang and Li 2020
)
, ours is more robust to model mis-specifications and outliers. For any pair
(\bm{\mathcal{T}},\bm{\mathcal{S}})\in(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha})
, a suitable loss function is the negative log-likelihood. By maximizing the log-likelihood, we define

\displaystyle(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\gamma},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\gamma}):=\argmin_{\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}}-\sum_{\omega}\big([\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\log p([\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega})+\big(1-[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\big)\log\big(1-p([\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega})\big)\big).

(2.2)

3
Estimating by Non-convex Optimization

Suppose that a pair
1
1

1

We will show, in Section
4
, that obtaining a good initialization for
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
is, under suitable conditions, easy once a good initialization for
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
is available.
of initializations near the ground truth is provided. Our estimating procedure adopts a gradient-based iterative algorithm to search for a local minimum of the loss. Since the problem is a constrained optimization, the major difficulty is on the enforcement of constraints during gradient descent updates. To ensure low-rankness, we apply the Riemannian gradient descent algorithm that is fast and simple to implement. Meanwhile, we enforce the sparsity constraint via a gradient-based pruning algorithm.

3.1
Riemannian Gradient Descent

Provided with
(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})
at the
l
-th iteration, the
vanilla
gradient of the loss function is
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}=\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})
. The naive gradient descent updates the low-rank part to
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\beta\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
with a carefully chosen stepsize
\beta>0
, and then projects it back into the set
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
. This procedure is sometimes referred to as the projected gradient descent (PGD)
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
.
Oftentimes, the gradient
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
has full ranks and thus the subsequent low-rank projection is computationally expensive. Observe that
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
is an element in the smooth manifold
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
. Meanwhile, due to the smoothness of loss function, it is well recognized that the optimization problem can be solved by Riemannian optimization
(
Edelman et al. 1998
;
Kressner et al. 2014
)
on the respective smooth manifold. Therefore, instead of using the vanilla gradient
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
, it suffices to take the
Riemannian gradient
, which corresponds to the steepest descent of the loss but is restricted to the tangent space of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
at the point
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
. The Riemannian gradient is low-rank rendering amazing computational speed-up. See numerical comparison in Section
7
.

An essential ingredient of Riemannian gradient descent is to project the vanilla gradient onto the tangent space of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
.
Let
\mathbb{T}_{l}
denote the tangent space of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
at
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
. Suppose that
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
admits a Tucker decomposition
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{l}\cdot\llbracket\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{l,1},\cdots,\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{l,m}\rrbracket
. The tangent space
\mathbb{T}_{l}

(
Cai et al. 2020
)
has an explicit form written as

\mathbb{T}_{l}=\big\{\bm{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\times_{i\in[m]}\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{l,i}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{l}\times_{j\in[m]\backslash i}\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{l,j}\times_{i}\mathbf{W}_{i}:\bm{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbf{r}},\mathbf{W}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}\times r_{i}},\mathbf{W}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{l,i}={\bf 0}\big\}
.
Clearly, all elements in
\mathbb{T}_{l}
has their multi-linear ranks upper bounded by
2\mathbf{r}
. Given the vanilla gradient
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
, its projection onto
\mathbb{T}_{l}
is defined by
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}):=\argmin_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{T}_{l}}\|\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
. The summands in
\mathbb{T}_{l}
are all orthogonal to each other, allowing fast computation for
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l})
.

By choosing a suitable stepsize
\beta>0
, the update by Riemannian gradient descent yields

\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}:=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\beta{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}.
But
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}
may fail to be an element in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
. To enforce the low-rank constraint, another key step in Riemannian optimization is the so-called
retraction
, which projects a general tensor
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}
back to the smooth manifold
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
. This procedure amounts to a low-rank approximation of the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}
. In addition, we also need to enforce the spikiness (or incoherent) condition on the low-rank estimate. Towards that end, we first truncate
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}
entry-wisely by
\zeta_{l+1}/2
for some easily chosen threshold
\zeta_{l+1}
and obtain
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}
, and then retract the truncated tensor
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}
back to the manifold
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
. We show that a low-rank approximation of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}
by a simple higher order singular value decomposition (HOSVD) guarantees the convergence of Riemannian gradient descent algorithm. More specifically, for all
j\in[m]
, compute
\mathbf{V}_{l,j}
which is the top-
r_{j}
left singular vectors of
{\cal M}_{j}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l})
. The HOSVD approximation of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}
with multi-linear ranks
\mathbf{r}
is obtained by

\mathscr{H}^{{\rm HO}}_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}):=(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}\times_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{V}_{l,j}^{\top})\cdot\llbracket\mathbf{V}_{l,1},\cdots,\mathbf{V}_{l,m}\rrbracket
.
Basically, retraction by HOSVD is the generalization of low-rank matrix approximation by singular value thresholding, although HOSVD is generally not the optimal low-rank approximation of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}_{l}
. See, e.g.
(
Zhang and Xia 2018
;
Xia and Zhou 2019
;
Liu et al. 2017
;
Richard and Montanari 2014
)
for more explanations. Now put these two steps together and we define a trimming operator
\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta,\mathbf{r}}
.

\displaystyle\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta,\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}):=\mathscr{H}^{{\rm HO}}_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}),\quad\textrm{ where }[\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{W}}}]_{\omega}=\begin{cases}(\zeta/2)\cdot{\rm Sign}([\bm{\mathcal{W}}]_{\omega}),&\textrm{ if }|[\bm{\mathcal{W}}]_{\omega}|>\zeta/2\\
[\bm{\mathcal{W}}]_{\omega},&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(3.1)

Equipped by the retraction and the entry-wise truncation, the Riemannian gradient descent algorithm updates the low-rank estimate by

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}=\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta_{l+1},\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}),

with a properly chosen
\zeta_{l+1}
.

3.2
Gradient Pruning

The next step is to update the estimate of sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
.
Provided with the updated
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
at the
l
-th iteration, an ideal estimator of the sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
is to find

\argmin_{\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}}\ \mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}).

Solving this problem is NP-hard for a general loss function.
Interestingly, if the loss function is entry-wise meaning that
\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})=\sum_{\omega}\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{\omega})
where
\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
for each
\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]
, the computation of sparse estimate becomes tractable.
More exactly, given a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
, we denote
|\bm{\mathcal{G}}|^{(n)}
the value of its
n
-th largest entry in absolute value for
\forall n\in[d_{1}\cdots d_{m}]
. Thus,
|\bm{\mathcal{G}}|^{(1)}
denotes its largest entry in absolute value. The
level-
\alpha
active indices
of
\bm{\mathcal{G}}
is defined by

\textsf{Level-$\alpha$ AInd}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}):=\big\{\omega=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}):\big|[\bm{\mathcal{G}}]_{\omega}\big|\geq\max\nolimits_{j\in[m]}\big|\mathbf{e}_{i_{j}}^{\top}{\cal M}_{j}(\bm{\mathcal{G}})\big|^{(\lfloor\alpha d_{j}^{-}\rfloor)}\big\}.

By definition, the
level-
\alpha
active indices
of
\bm{\mathcal{G}}
are those entries whose absolute value is no smaller than the
(1-\alpha)
-th percentile in absolute value on each of its corresponding slices. Clearly, for any
\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}
, the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
belongs to the
\textsf{Level-$\alpha$ AInd}(\bm{\mathcal{S}})
.

We compute the vanilla gradient
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{l}=\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})
so that
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{l}]_{\omega}=\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}_{\omega}([\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}]_{\omega})
and find
\mathbb{J}=\textsf{Level-$\alpha$ AInd}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{l})
. The indices in
\mathbb{J}
have the greatest potential in decreasing the value of loss function. The gradient pruning algorithm sets
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}=0
if
\omega\notin\mathbb{J}
. On the other hand, for
\omega\in\mathbb{J}
, ideally, the entry
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}
is chosen to vanish the gradient in that
\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}^{\prime}\big([\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}\big)=0
. However, for functions with always-positive gradient (e.g.
e^{x}
), it is impossible to vanish the gradient. Generally, we choose a pruning parameter
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}>0
and set

\displaystyle[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}:=\argmin\nolimits_{s:|s+[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}]_{\omega}|\leq\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}}\ \big|\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}^{\prime}([\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}]_{\omega}+s)\big|,\quad\forall\omega\in\mathbb{J}.

(3.2)

Basically, eq. (
3.2
) chooses
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}
from the closed interval
\big[-\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}-[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}]_{\omega},\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}-[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}]_{\omega}\big]
to minimize the gradient.
For a properly selected loss function
\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}(\cdot)
, searching for the solution
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}
is usually fast.
Moreover, for entry-wise square loss, the pruning parameter
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
can be
\infty
and
[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}
has a closed-form solution. See Section
5
for more details.
The procedure of gradient pruning is summarized in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Gradient Pruning for Sparse Estimate

Input:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
and parameters
\gamma>1,\alpha,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}>0

Calculate the gradient
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{l}=\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})
and find
\mathbb{J}=\textsf{Level-$\gamma\alpha$ AInd}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{l})

for

\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]

do

[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}=\begin{cases}\textrm{by (\ref{eq:prune})},&\textrm{ if }\omega\in\mathbb{J}\\
0,&\textrm{ if }\omega\notin\mathbb{J}\end{cases}

end

for

Output:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}

Final algorithm
.
Putting together the Riemannian gradient descent and the gradient pruning algorithm,
we propose the following Algorithm
2
. The algorithm alternatingly updates the low-rank estimate and the sparse estimate. We emphasize that the notations
\alpha
and
\mu_{1}
in Algorithm
2
do not have to be exactly the model parameters
\alpha
and
\mu_{1}
. In theory, we only require them to be larger than the true model parameters
\alpha
and
\mu_{1}
, respectively. See Section
7
for more details.

Algorithm 2

Riemannian Gradient Descent and Gradient Pruning

Initialization:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
, stepsize
\beta
and parameters
\alpha,\gamma,\mu_{1},\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}>0

Apply Algorithm
1
with input
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
and parameters
\alpha,\gamma,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
to obtain
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{0}

for

l=0,1,\cdots,l_{\max}-1

do

\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}=\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})

\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}

\zeta_{l+1}=\frac{16}{7}\mu_{1}\frac{\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}\|_{\rm F}}{\sqrt{d^{*}}}

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}=\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta_{l+1},\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l})

Apply Algorithm
1
with input
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}
and parameters
\alpha,\gamma,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
to obtain
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l+1}

end

for

Output:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}

Rank, sparsity and algorithmic parameters selection
.
For applications where the true ranks are small, we can simply run Algorithm
2
for multiple times with distinct choices of these ranks and decide the best ones according to certain criterion, e.g., interpretability if no ground truth
(
Jing et al. 2020
;
Fan et al. 2021
)
or the mis-clustering rate if ground truth is available
(
Ke et al. 2019
;
Zhou et al. 2013
;
Wang and Li 2020
)
. Sometimes, it suffices to take the singular values of the matricizations and decide the cut-off point by the famous
scree plot

(
Cattell 1966
)
. For generalized linear models, selecting the best
\mathbf{r}
and
\alpha
is challenging. Nevertheless, we suggest to minimize the following BIC-type criterion:

{\rm BIC}(\mathbf{r},\alpha):=\big(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}\|_{\ell_{0}}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}d_{i}\big)\cdot\ln(d^{\ast})-2\ln(\widehat{L}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha})

(3.3)

where
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}
is the estimated sparse tensor and
\widehat{L}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}
denotes the respective value of likelihood function, i.e.,
-2\ln(\widehat{L}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha})=d^{\ast}\log(\|\bm{\mathcal{A}}-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}\|_{\rm F}^{2})
for Example
2.1
(assuming Gaussian noise with unknown variance) and
-\ln(\widehat{L}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha})
is the RHS of (
2.2
) for Example
2.2
. Criterion (
3.3
) works reasonably well for Example
2.1
and
2.2
, and yields interesting outcomes on international commodity trade flows data. We also propose practical guideline on choosing the algorithmic parameters
\gamma,\mu_{1}
and
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
. See Section
7
and the supplement for more details.

4
General Convergence and Statistical Guarantees

In this section, we investigate the local convergence of Algorithm
2
in a general framework, and characterize the error of final estimates in terms of the gradient of loss function. Their applications on more specific examples are collected in Section
5
. Our theory relies crucially on the regularity of loss function. Recall that Algorithm
2
involves: routine 1. Riemannian gradient descent for the low-rank estimate; and routine 2. gradient pruning for the sparse estimate. It turns out that these two routines generally require different regularity conditions on the loss function, although these conditions can be equivalent in special cases (e.g. see Section
5.1
). Recall that
\gamma>1
is the tuning parameter in Algorithm
2
which
only
plays a role in
\gamma\alpha
, i.e., the desired sparsity.

Assumption 2
.

(Needed for Low-rank Estimate)
There exist
b_{l},b_{u}>0
such that
\mathfrak{L}(\cdot)
is
b_{l}
-strongly convex and
b_{u}
-smooth in a subset
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}\subset\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\}
meaning that

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\mathcal{X}}-(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}),\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})-\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\rangle\geq b_{l}\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})-\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\|_{\rm F}\leq b_{u}\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}

(4.2)

for all
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
. Note that
b_{l}
and
b_{u}
may depend on
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
.

Note that the explicit form of subset
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
in Assumption
2
is usually determined by the actual problems (see examples in Section
5
). For the main theorem in this section (Theorem
4.1
), we consider
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
to be a small neighbour around the truth
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. In this case,
Assumption
2
requires the loss function to be
locally
strongly convex and smooth.

Assumption 3
.

(Needed for Sparse Estimate)
Suppose that
\mathfrak{L}
is an entry-wise loss meaning
\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})=\sum_{\omega}\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{T}}]_{\omega})
where
\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
for any
\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]
. There exist a subset
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}\subset\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\}
and
b_{l},b_{u}>0
such that

\displaystyle\langle[\bm{\mathcal{X}}]_{\omega}-[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega},\nabla\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{X}}]_{\omega})

\displaystyle-\nabla\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega})\rangle\geq b_{l}|[\bm{\mathcal{X}}-\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}|^{2}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\big|\nabla\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{X}}]_{\omega})-\nabla\mathfrak{l}_{\omega}([\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega})\big|

\displaystyle\leq b_{u}\big|[\bm{\mathcal{X}}-\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}\big|

(4.4)

for
\forall\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]
and any
\bm{\mathcal{X}},\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathbb{B}^{\ast}_{\infty}
. Similarly,
b_{l}
and
b_{u}
may depend on
\mathbb{B}^{\ast}_{\infty}
.

The gradient pruning Algorithm
1
operates on entries of the gradients. Intuitively, entry-wise loss not only simplifies the computation but also helps characterize the performance of gradient pruning algorithm. If the sparse component is absent in our model, i.e. the underlying tensor is exactly low-rank, Assumption
3
will be unnecessary. See Section
6
in the supplement for more details.
Notice that the same parameters
b_{l},b_{u}
are both used in Assumption
2
and Assumption
3
. This slightly abuse of notations is for the ease of exposition. These parameters are not necessarily equal.

For an entry-wise loss, condition (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) imply the condition (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), respectively. Therefore, Assumption
2
can be a by-product of Assumption
3
, if we ignore the possible differences between the two neighbours
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
. In this way, these two assumptions can be merged into one single assumption. However, we state them separately for several purposes. First, they highlight the differences of theoretical requirements between Riemannian gradient descent and gradient pruning algorithms. Second, the neighbours in these assumptions (
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
) can be drastically different. Third, keeping them separate eases subsequent applications for special cases (e.g., for exact low-rank estimate in Section
6
).

The signal strength
\underline{\lambda}
is defined by
\underline{\lambda}:=\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}):=\min_{j\in[m]}\lambda_{r_{j}}\big({\cal M}_{j}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast})\big)
. Here
\lambda_{r}(\cdot)
denotes the
r
-th largest singular value of a matrix. Thus,
\underline{\lambda}
represents the smallest non-zero singular value among all the matricizations of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
. Similarly, denote
\overline{\lambda}:=\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}):=\max_{j\in[m]}\lambda_{1}\big({\cal M}_{j}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast})\big)
and define
\kappa_{0}:=\overline{\lambda}\underline{\lambda}^{-1}
to be the condition number of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
.

Define

\displaystyle\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}:=\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathbb{M}_{2\mathbf{r}},\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|_{\rm F}\leq 1}\big<\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}),\bm{\mathcal{M}}\big>

(4.5)

and

\textsf{Err}_{\infty}:=\max\big\{\|\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})\|_{\ell_{\infty}},\min\nolimits_{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}}\|\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\big\},

where
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
is the tuning parameter in gradient pruning Algorithm
1
.
The quantity
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}
is typical in the aforementioned literature in exact low-rank matrix and tensor estimation. But the special quantity
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
appears in our paper for investigating the performance of gradient pruning algorithm. The first term in
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
comes from the gradient of loss function at the ground truth characterizing the stochastic error in many statistical models, while the second term is due to the setting of tuning parameter
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
.

Theorem
4.1
displays the general performance bounds of Algorithm
2
. For simplicity, we denote
\Omega^{\ast}
the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
,
\bar{r}=\max_{j}r_{j},\bar{d}=\max_{j}d_{j},\underline{d}=\min_{j}d_{j},r^{\ast}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
and
d^{\ast}:=d_{j}d_{j}^{-}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
.
Let
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
denote the vectorized
\ell_{\infty}
-norm of tensors.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\gamma>1,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}>0
be the parameters used in Algorithm
2
.
Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold with
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}=\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq C_{0,m}\underline{\lambda},\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}=\big\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\textsf{k}_{\infty},\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\big\}
where
\textsf{k}_{\infty}=C_{1,m}\mu_{1}^{2m}(\bar{r}^{m-1}/\underline{d}^{m-1})^{1/2}\underline{\lambda}+\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}+\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
,
0.36b_{l}(b_{u}^{2})^{-1}\leq 1
and
b_{u}b_{l}^{-1}\leq 0.4(\sqrt{\delta})^{-1}
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and large absolute constants
C_{0,m},C_{1,m}>0
depending only on
m
. Assume that

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\big\}
,
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:

\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}/\underline{\lambda}+\textsf{Err}_{\infty}(b_{u}+1)(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})^{1/2}/(b_{l}\underline{\lambda})\leq c_{2,m}\cdot\min\Big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\Big\}

(c)

Sparsity condition:
\alpha\leq c_{3,m}(\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}b_{u}^{4}b_{l}^{-4})^{-1}
and
\gamma\geq 1+(4m)^{-1}b_{u}^{4}b_{l}^{-4}

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m},c_{3,m}>0
are small constants depending only on
m
. If the stepsize
\beta
is between
[0.005b_{l}/(b_{u}^{2}),0.36b_{l}/(b_{u}^{2})]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta^{2})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{1,\delta}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}+C_{1,b_{u},b_{l}}\left(|\Omega^{*}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast}\right)\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l+1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\left(C_{2,m}\frac{1}{\gamma-1}+C_{3,m}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}\alpha\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

where
C_{1,\delta}=6\delta^{-1}
and
C_{1,b_{u},b_{l}}=(C_{2}+C_{3}b_{u}+C_{4}b_{u}^{2})b_{l}^{-2}
for absolute constants
C_{1},\cdots,C_{4}>0
and
C_{2,m},C_{3,m}>0
depending only on
m
.
Therefore, for all
l\in[l_{\max}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1,\delta}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}+C_{1,b_{u},b_{l}}\left(|\Omega^{*}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast}\right)\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}.

(4.7)

By eq. (
4.7
), after suitably chosen
l_{\max}
iterations and treating
b_{l},b_{u},\delta
as constants, we conclude with the following error bounds:

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{1}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}+C_{2}(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})\textsf{Err}^{2}_{\infty}

(4.8)

and

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\frac{\alpha(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}(\gamma-1)+1}{\gamma-1}\cdot\big(C_{5}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}+C_{6}(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})\textsf{Err}^{2}_{\infty}\big)+C_{7}(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})\textsf{Err}^{2}_{\infty}.

There exist two types of error as illustrated on the RHS of (
4.8
). The first term
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}
comes from the model complexity of low-rank
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
, and the term
|\Omega^{\ast}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}
is related to the model complexity of sparse
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. These two terms both reflect the intrinsic complexity of our model. On the other hand, the last term
\gamma\alpha d^{\ast}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}
is a human-intervened complexity which originates from the tuning parameter
\gamma
in the algorithm design. If the cardinality of
\Omega^{\ast}
happens to be of the same order as
\alpha d^{\ast}
(it is the worse-case cardinality of
\Omega^{\ast}
for
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}
), the error bound is simplified into the following corollary. It is an immediate result from Theorem
4.1
and we hence omit the proof.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
4.1
hold and assume that
|\Omega^{*}|\asymp\alpha d^{\ast}
. Then for all
l=1,\cdots,l_{\max}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1,\delta}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}+C_{1,b_{u},b_{l}}|\Omega^{*}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}.

Remarks on the conditions of Theorem
4.1
.
The initialization is required to be as close to
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
as
o(\underline{\lambda})
, if
b_{l},b_{u},\kappa_{0}
and
\bar{r}
are all
O(1)
constants. It is a common condition for non-convex methods for low-rank matrix and tensor related problems. Concerning the signal-to-noise ratio condition, Theorem
4.1
requires
\underline{\lambda}
to dominate
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}
and
(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})^{1/2}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
if
b_{l},b_{u},\kappa_{0},\bar{r}=O(1)
. This condition is mild and perhaps minimal. The sparsity requirement on
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
is also mild. Assuming
b_{l},b_{u},\kappa_{0},\bar{r},\mu_{1}=O(1)
, Theorem
4.1
merely requires
\alpha\leq c
for a sufficiently small
c>0
which depends only on
m
, implying that Algorithm
2
allows a wide range of sparsity on
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. Similarly, Theorem
4.1
only requires
\gamma\geq C
for a sufficiently large
C>0
which depends on
m
only.

We now investigate the recovery of the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. Algorithm
2
usually over-estimates the size of the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
since the Level-
{\gamma\alpha}
active indices are used for a
\gamma
strictly greater than
1
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Suppose conditions of Theorem
4.1
hold,
b_{l},b_{u}=O(1)
,
|\Omega^{\ast}|\asymp\alpha d^{\ast}
and
l_{\max}
is chosen such that (
4.8
) holds. Then,

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq C_{1,m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\Big(\frac{\bar{r}^{m}}{\underline{d}^{m-1}}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\big(\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}+(\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)^{1/2}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\big)+C_{2,m}\textsf{Err}_{\infty},

(4.9)

where
C_{1,m},C_{2,m}>0
only depend on
m
.

If the non-zero entries of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
satisfy
|[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}|>2\delta^{\ast}
for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\ast}
where
\delta^{\ast}
is the RHS of (
4.9
), we obtain
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
by a final-stage hard thresholding on
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}
so that

[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}]_{\omega}:=[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}]_{\omega}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\big(|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}]_{\omega}|>\delta^{\ast}\big).

By Theorem
4.3
, we get
{\rm supp}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}})=\Omega^{\ast}
and thus recovering the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
. The lower bound on the outliers is necessary for distinguishing the noise and outliers. If an entry in the outliers is of small magnitude, then it might be considered as noise. When the noise does not exist, we can set
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=\infty
implying
\delta^{\ast}=0
.

5
Applications

We now apply the established results in Section
4
to more specific examples and elaborate the respective statistical performances. Our framework certainly covers many other interesting examples but we do not intend to exhaust them.

5.1
Sub-Gaussian Tensor Robust PCA with i.i.d. Noise

As introduced in Example
2.1
, the goal of SG-RPCA is to extract low-rank
signal
from a noisy tensor observation
\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
. Due to the linearity, we use the loss function
\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}):=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}-\bm{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
. Clearly, this loss is an entry-wise loss function, and satisfies the strongly-convex and smoothness conditions of Assumptions
2
and
3
with constants
b_{l}=b_{u}=1
within any subsets
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
, or simply
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}=\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
.
As a result, Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.3
are readily applicable by choosing
\delta=0.15
, and setting the tuning parameter
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=\infty
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and there exists
\sigma_{z}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\exp\{t[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}\}\leq\exp\{t^{2}\sigma_{z}^{2}/2\}
for
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\forall\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]
. Let
r^{\ast}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
and
\gamma>1
be the tuning parameter in Algorithm
2
. Assume
|\Omega^{\ast}|\asymp\alpha d^{\ast}
and

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\underline{\lambda}\cdot(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:
\underline{\lambda}/\sigma_{z}\geq C_{1,m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2}\cdot(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\log\bar{d})^{1/2}

(c)

Sparsity condition:
\alpha\leq c_{2,m}(\mu_{1}^{4m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m})^{-1}
and
\gamma\geq 1+4m

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m},C_{1,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
. If the step size
\beta\in[0.005,0.36]
, then after
l_{\max}>1
iterations, with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 0.98^{l_{\max}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{2,m}(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\log\bar{d})\sigma_{z}^{2}

(5.1)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(C_{3,m}\alpha\bar{r}^{m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}+C_{4,m}(\gamma-1)^{-1}\right)\cdot\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{5,m}\sigma_{z}^{2}\cdot\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\log\bar{d}

where
C_{2,m},C_{3,m},C_{4,m},C_{5,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
. Moreover, If
l_{\max}
is chosen large enough such that the second term on RHS of (
5.1
) dominates and assume
\mu_{1}^{4m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}(\bar{r}\bar{d}+r^{\ast})\leq C_{9,m}\underline{d}^{m-1}
, we get with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle\left(C_{6,m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\bar{r}^{m/2}(\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)^{1/2}/\underline{d}^{(m-1)/2}+C_{7,m}\right)\cdot\sigma_{z}\log^{1/2}\bar{d}

where
C_{6,m},C_{7,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
.

Theorem
5.1
has several interesting implications. If the noise is absent meaning
\sigma_{z}=0
, eq. (
5.1
) implies that, for an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, after
l_{\max}\asymp\log(\varepsilon^{-1})
iterations, Algorithm
2
outputs a
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
satisfying
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}=O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore, Algorithm
2
can exactly recover the low-rank and sparse component, separately.
On the other hand, if
\sigma_{z}>0
and
l_{\max}\asymp\log(\underline{\lambda}\sigma_{z}^{-1})
, eq. (
5.1
) implies that Algorithm
2
produces, with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{2,m}\sigma_{z}^{2}(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\log\bar{d}).

(5.2)

Since the intrinsic model complexity is of order
\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+|\Omega^{\ast}|
, the bound (
5.2
) is sharp up to logarithmic factors. Similar bounds also hold for
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
. In addition, if
\mu_{1}^{4m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)\leq C_{9,m}\underline{d}^{m-1}
, we get with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq C_{7,m}\sigma_{z}\log^{1/2}\bar{d}.

(5.3)

Bound (
5.3
) is nearly optimal. To see it, consider the simpler model that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}={\bf 0}
. Then, it is equivalent to estimate a sparse tensor from the data
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
. Without further information,
O(\sigma_{z}\log^{1/2}\bar{d})
is the best sup-norm performance one can expect in general.

Comparison with existing literature.

In
Lu et al. 2016
;
Zhou and Feng 2017
, the authors studied noiseless RPCA assuming low tubal rank and proved that a convex program can exactly recover the underlying parameters. Their method works only for third order tensor and is not applicable to low Tucker-rank tensors, and there exists no statistical guarantee for the noisy setting.
In
Gu et al. 2014
, the authors proposed the convex relaxation by unfolding a tensor into matrices. Their method is statistically sub-optimal and computationally more demanding. See Table
1
in Introduction.
Interestingly, by setting
|\Omega^{\ast}|=0
, our Theorem
5.1
degrades to well-established results for tensor PCA in the literature, e.g., higher order orthogonal iteration in
Zhang and Xia 2018
and regularized jointly gradient descent in
Han et al. 2020
.

Initialization.

We verify that the initialization conditions required by Theorem
5.1
can be satisfied under mild conditions. Recall that we denote the
\lfloor pd^{*}\rfloor
-th largest entry of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
in absolute value by
|\bm{\mathcal{A}}|^{(\lfloor pd^{*}\rfloor)}
.
The idea of the initialization process is to first truncate the observed tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
defined by
[\textsf{Trunc}_{\tau}(\bm{\mathcal{A}})]_{\omega}=\tau\cdot{\rm Sign}([\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(|[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}|>\tau)+[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(|[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}|\leq\tau)
.
We then apply the higher-order orthogonal iteration (HOOI) to
[\textsf{Trunc}_{\tau}(\bm{\mathcal{A}})]_{\omega}
. The choice of
\tau
is given in Algorithm
3
. The details of HOOI can be found in the supplement. We remark that, in practice, the spikiness-related parameter
\mu_{1}
can be set to
2^{m}+\log\bar{d}
and gradually double it if Algorithm
2
fails to converge. The theoretical guarantee is provided by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, the support of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
is
\Omega^{*}
with cardinality
|\Omega^{*}|
,
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
has i.i.d entries with
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]^{2}_{\omega}=\sigma_{z}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}\exp\{t[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}\}\leq\exp\{c_{1}t^{2}\sigma_{z}^{2}\}
for
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
and some absolute constant
c_{1}>0
. There exist absolute constants
C_{1,m},C_{2,m},C_{3,m},c_{2,m}>0
such that if the maximum iteration of
HOOI

t_{\max}\geq C_{1,m}\big(\log(\bar{d}\kappa_{0})\vee 1\big)
and

(a)

Sparsity condition:
|\Omega^{*}|\leq c_{2,m}\kappa_{0}^{-4m-2}\bar{r}^{-2}\mu_{1}^{-4}\log^{-2}(\bar{d})\cdot\min\{(\underline{\lambda}/\sigma_{z})^{2},d^{*}\}
,

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:
\underline{\lambda}/\sigma_{z}\geq C_{2,m}\max\{\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2}\mu_{1}[(r^{*})^{1/2}+(\bar{d}\bar{r})^{1/2}]\log(\bar{d}),(d^{*})^{1/4}\}
.,

the output of Algorithm
3
satisfies the initialization condition in Theorem
5.1
with probability at least
1-C_{3,m}\bar{d}^{-2}
.

Algorithm 3

Initialization for SG-RPCA

Take
p=\min\{(8\mu_{1}^{2})^{-1},(64m\log\bar{d})^{-1}\}
, and set
\tau_{0}=|\bm{\mathcal{A}}|^{(\lfloor pd^{*}\rfloor)}
.

Define
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
by
[\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{0}]_{\omega}=\begin{cases}&[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega},\text{~if~}|[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}|\leq\tau_{0}\\
&0,\text{~otherwise.}\end{cases}

Set
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}=\textsf{Trunc}_{\tau}(\bm{\mathcal{A}})
with
\tau=10\sqrt{m\log(\bar{d})}\mu_{1}\|\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\|_{\rm F}/\sqrt{d^{*}}
.

(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}};\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{m})=\textrm{HOOI}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{A}}})
.

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}=\textsf{Trim}_{\eta,\mathbf{r}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
with
\eta=16\mu_{1}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\|_{\rm F}/(7\sqrt{d^{*}})
.

5.2
Tensor PCA under Heavy-tailed Noise

Most aforementioned literature in Section
5.1
on tensor PCA focus on sub-Gaussian
(
Vershynin 2011
;
Vershynin 2018
;
Pan et al. 2018
;
Li et al. 2018
)
. Nowadays, heavy-tailed noise routinely arise in diverse fields. However, the performances of most existing approaches for tensor PCA significantly deteriorate when noise have heavy tails. Interestingly, tensor PCA under heavy-tailed noise can be regarded as a special case of SG-RPCA. Suppose that the observed tensorial data
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
satisfies
\bm{\mathcal{A}}=\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
with
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}}
. The noise tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
satisfies the following tail assumption.

Assumption 4
.

(
\theta
-tailed noise)
The entries of
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
are
i.i.d.
with
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}=0
and
{\rm Var}([\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega})\leq\sigma_{z}^{2}
. There exist
\theta>2
such that
\mathbb{P}(|[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}|\geq\sigma_{z}\cdot t)\leq t^{-\theta}
for all
t>1
.

If
\theta
is only moderately large (e.g.,
\theta=3
), many entries of
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
can have large magnitudes such that the typical concentration properties of
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
(e.g., the bounds of
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}
and
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
in Section
5.1
) disappear.
Fix any
\alpha>1
, we decompose
[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}=[\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}]_{\omega}+[\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{Z}}}]_{\omega}
such that

[\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{Z}}}]_{\omega}=\mathbbm{1}(|[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}|\geq\alpha\sigma_{z})\cdot(\alpha\sigma_{z})\textrm{sign}([\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega})+\mathbbm{1}(|[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}|<\alpha\sigma_{z})\cdot[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega},\quad\forall\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}].

By definition, the entry
[\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}]_{\omega}\neq 0
if and only if
|[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}|>\alpha\sigma_{z}
. Now, we write

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{A}}=\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}+\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{Z}}}

(5.4)

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose Assumption
4
holds and the distribution of
[\bm{\mathcal{Z}}]_{\omega}
is symmetric. For any
\alpha>1
, we have
\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{Z}}}={\bf 0}
. There exists an event
\mathfrak{E}_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{E}_{1})\geq 1-\bar{d}^{-2}
such that
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
in the event
\mathfrak{E}_{1}
where
\alpha^{\prime}=\max\{2\alpha^{-\theta},10(\bar{d}/d^{\ast})\log(m\bar{d}^{3})\}
.

By Lemma
5.3
, in the event
\mathfrak{E}_{1}
, model (
5.4
) satisfies the SG-RPCA model such that
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Meanwhile, the entries of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{Z}}}
are sub-Gaussian for being uniformly bounded by
\alpha\sigma_{z}
. Therefore, conditioned on
\mathfrak{E}_{1}
, Theorem
5.1
is readily applicable.

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose Assumption
1
and the conditions of Lemma
5.3
hold. Choose
\alpha\asymp(d^{\ast}/\bar{d})^{1/\theta},\gamma>1
as the tuning parameters in Algorithm
2
. Assume
(\bar{d}/d^{\ast})\log(m\bar{d}^{3})\leq c_{2,m}(\mu_{1}^{4m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m})^{-1}
,
\gamma\geq 1+4m
and

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\underline{\lambda}\cdot(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:
\underline{\lambda}/(\alpha\sigma_{z})\geq C_{1,m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2}\cdot\big(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma\bar{d}\log(m\bar{d})\big)^{1/2}

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m},C_{1,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
. If the step size
\beta\in[0.005,0.36]
, then after
l_{\max}>1
iterations, with probability at least
1-2\bar{d}^{-2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 0.98^{l_{\max}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{2,m}\gamma\big(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\bar{d}\log(m\bar{d})\big)\alpha^{2}\sigma_{z}^{2},

(5.5)

where
C_{2,m}>0
is a constant depending only on
m
.

By Theorem
5.4
and (
5.5
), if
\kappa_{0},\gamma=O(1)
,
d_{j}\asymp d
with
\alpha\asymp d^{(m-1)/\theta}
and
l_{\max}
is properly chosen, we get with probability at least
1-2d^{-2}
that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{2,m}\big(d\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+d\log(md)\big)d^{2(m-1)/\theta}\cdot\sigma_{z}^{2}.

(5.6)

The bound (
5.6
) decreases as
\theta
increases implying that the final estimate
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
becomes more accurate as the noise tail gets lighter.
Moreover, if Assumption
4
holds with
\theta=2(m-1)\log d
so that
d^{2(m-1)/\theta}=O(1)
, bound (
5.6
) implies
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}/\sigma_{z}^{2}=O\big(d\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+d\log(md)\big)
which is sharp up to logarithmic factors. Similarly as Theorem
5.1
, under Assumption
1
, it is possible to derive an
\ell_{\infty}
-norm bound for
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
.

Initialization
.
The initialization can be attained by Algorithm
3
. Under bounded spikiness condition, we initially take
\mu_{1}=2^{m}+\log(\bar{d})
and gradually double it if Algorithm
2
fails to converge. Similarly by Lemma
5.2
, we have the following initialization guarantee for heavy-tailed tensor PCA.

Lemma 5.5
.

Under the setting of Theorem
5.4
, there exist absolute constants
C_{1,m},C_{2,m},C_{3,m}>0
such that if the signal-noise-ratio

\underline{\lambda}/(\alpha\sigma_{z})\geq C_{1,m}\max\left\{\kappa_{0}^{2m+1}\mu_{1}^{2}\bar{r}^{1/2}[(r^{*})^{1/2}+(\bar{d}\bar{r})^{1/2}]\log^{2}(\bar{d}),(d^{*})^{1/4}\right\},

after
t_{\max}\geq C_{2,m}\big(\log(\bar{d}\kappa_{0})\vee 1\big)
iterations, the output of Algorithm
3
satisfies the initialization condition in Theorem
5.4
with probability at least
1-C_{3,m}\bar{d}^{-2}
.

5.3
Bernoulli Tensor Robust PCA

Following the Bernoulli tensor model in Example
2.2
, based on a binary tensorial observation
\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
, we choose the loss function to be the
negative
log-likelihood (without loss of generality, we set the scale parameter
\sigma=1
for ease of exposition)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}})=-\sum\nolimits_{\omega}\Big([\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\log p([\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega})+(1-[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega})\log\big(1-p([\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega})\big)\Big).

(5.7)

The RHS of (
5.7
) is an entry-wise loss, and Assumptions
2
and
3
are determined by the entry-wise second order derivatives. For
\forall\zeta>0
, define

b_{u,\zeta}:=\max\bigg\{\sup_{|x|\leq\zeta}\frac{(p^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{p^{2}(x)}-\frac{p^{\prime\prime}(x)}{p(x)},\ \sup_{|x|\leq\zeta}\frac{(p^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{(1-p(x))^{2}}+\frac{p^{\prime\prime}(x)}{1-p(x)}\bigg\}

b_{l,\zeta}:=\min\bigg\{\inf_{|x|\leq\zeta}\frac{(p^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{p^{2}(x)}-\frac{p^{\prime\prime}(x)}{p(x)},\ \inf_{|x|\leq\zeta}\frac{(p^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{(1-p(x))^{2}}+\frac{p^{\prime\prime}(x)}{1-p(x)}\bigg\}

Assuming
b_{l,\zeta},b_{u,\zeta}>0
, then the loss function (
5.7
) satisfies Assumptions
2
and
3
with constants
b_{l,\zeta}
and
b_{u,\zeta}
for
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}=\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}=\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta,\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\}
(more precisely, the low-rank and sparse conditions are unnecessary).

Notice that
b_{l,\zeta}
and
b_{u,\zeta}
can be extremely sensitive to large
\zeta
. For instance
(
Wang and Li 2020
)
, we have

\displaystyle b_{l,\zeta}=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{\zeta}}{(1+e^{\zeta})^{2}},&\textrm{ if }p(x)=(1+e^{-x})^{-1}\\
\gtrsim\frac{\zeta+1/6}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\zeta^{2}},&\textrm{ if }p(x)=\Phi(x)\end{cases}\quad{\rm and}\quad b_{u,\zeta}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4},&\textrm{ if }p(x)=(1+e^{-x})^{-1}\\
\geq 0.6,&\textrm{ if }p(x)=\Phi(x)\end{cases}

implying that
b_{u,\zeta}b_{l,\zeta}^{-1}
increases very fast as
\zeta
becomes larger. Toward that end, we impose the following assumption which implies
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta/2
so that
\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}+\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta
.

Assumption 5
.

There exists a small
\zeta>0
such that
\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta/2
,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
satisfies Assumption
1
and its largest singular value

\overline{\lambda}\leq c_{m}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{-m}(\sqrt{d^{*}/r^{*}})\cdot\zeta

where
r^{\ast}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
and
d^{\ast}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
.

Meanwhile, we shall guarantee that the iterates
(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})
produced by our algorithm satisfy
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}\|_{\ell_{\infty}},\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=O(\zeta)
. The infinity norm bound for the sparse part is ensured by the choice of
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
.
And the low rank part is guaranteed by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
5
hold. Given any
\bm{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
, if we choose
\eta=16\mu_{1}\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}\|_{\rm F}/(7\sqrt{d^{*}})
, then
\|\textsf{Trim}_{\eta,\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq(9\zeta/16)\cdot(\kappa_{0}\mu_{1})^{m}
.

By Lemma
5.6
, if
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1},m=O(1)
and
\big\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
, the trimming operator guarantees
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=O(\zeta)
. Equipped with (
3.1
) and by setting
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=C_{1}\zeta
for some absolute
C_{1}>1
depending only on
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1}
and
m
, we apply Algorithm
2
to minimize the RHS of (
5.7
).

Similarly, the error of final estimate relies on
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}
and
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
, both of which are related to the gradient of loss (
5.7
). Denote
L_{\zeta}:=\sup\nolimits_{|x|\leq\zeta}|p^{\prime}(x)|/|p(x)\big(1-p(x)\big)|.

Since
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=C_{1}\zeta>\zeta
, by definition of
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\leq\max\bigg\{L_{\zeta},\min_{|x|\leq\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}}\bigg|\frac{p^{\prime}(x)}{p(x)\big(1-p(x)\big)}\bigg|\bigg\}\leq L_{\zeta}.

(5.8)

In practice, due to sparsity, the value
\zeta
is often small and it suffices to set
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=1
in Algorithm
2
and for a cleaner bound of Theorem
5.7
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\gamma>1,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}:=C_{1}\zeta
be the parameters used in Algorithm
2
for a constant
C_{1}>1
depending only on
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1}
and
m
via Lemma
5.6
.
Suppose Assumptions
1
and
5
hold. Assume
|\Omega^{\ast}|\asymp\alpha d^{\ast}
,
0.36b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}^{-2}\leq 1
,
b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}^{-1}\leq 0.4(\sqrt{\delta})^{-1}
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\zeta^{\prime}=(2C_{1}+1)\zeta
, and

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\big\}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq c_{2,m}\zeta

and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:

\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\big\}\geq C_{2,m}\Big(\sqrt{\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\frac{1+b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}}{b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}}\Big)\cdot L_{\zeta}

(c)

Sparsity condition:
\alpha\leq c_{3,m}b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}^{4}(b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}^{4}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m})^{-1}
and
\gamma\geq 1+4m\cdot b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}^{4}b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}^{-4}

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m},c_{3,m},C_{2,m}>0
are some constants depending on
m
only. If the stepsize
\beta\in[0.005b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}/(b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}})^{2},0.36b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}/(b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}})^{2}]
, after
l_{\max}
iterations, with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{l_{\max}}\cdot\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{3}L_{\zeta}^{2}\cdot(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)

(5.9)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}}^{2}}{b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}^{2}}\big(C_{4,m}\alpha\bar{r}^{m}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}+C_{5,m}(\gamma-1)^{-1}\big)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{6,m}}{b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}}^{2}}L_{\zeta}^{2}\cdot\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|

where
C_{3}>0
depends only on
\delta,b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}},b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}},m
, and
C_{4,m},C_{5,m},C_{6,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
. Moreover, if
l_{\max}
is chosen large enough such that the second term on RHS of (
5.9
) dominates and assume
\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}(\bar{r}\bar{d}+r^{\ast})\lesssim_{m}O(\underline{d}^{m-1})
, we get with probability at least
1-\bar{d}^{-2}
that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{6}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}(\bar{r}^{m}/\underline{d}^{m-1})^{1/2}(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)^{1/2}\cdot L_{\zeta}

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle\Big(C_{7}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\bar{r}^{m/2}|\Omega^{\ast}|^{1/2}/\underline{d}^{(m-1)/2}+C_{8}\Big)\cdot L_{\zeta}

where
C_{6},C_{7},C_{8}>0
depend only on
\delta,b_{l,\zeta^{\prime}},b_{u,\zeta^{\prime}},m
.

By Theorem
5.7
, after a properly chosen
l_{\max}
iterations and treating
\gamma
as a bounded constant, bound (
5.9
) implies
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O\big(L_{\zeta}^{2}\cdot(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+|\Omega^{\ast}|)\big)
. Note that the term
\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast}+|\Omega^{\ast}|
is the model complexity and thus this rate is sharp in general. If
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}={\bf 0}
so that
|\Omega^{\ast}|=0
, this rate is comparable to the existing ones in generalized low-rank tensor estimation
(
Wang and Li 2020
;
Han et al. 2020
)
. Additionally, for the matrix case (
m=2
), this rate matches the well-known results in
(
Davenport et al. 2014
;
Robin et al. 2020
)
.

Initialization
.
The initialization for treating binary tensorial data can be obtained by unfolding
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
into a
(d_{1}\cdots d_{m_{0}})\times(d_{m_{0}+1}\cdots d_{m})
matrix and applying
1
bit matrix estimation
(
Davenport et al. 2014
)
. Here
m_{0}=\lfloor m/2\rfloor
. It is based on the solution to a convex program. See the supplement for more details. Its theoretical performance is guaranteed by Lemma
5.8
. We write
d_{1}^{\ast}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m_{0}}
,
d_{2}^{\ast}=d_{m_{0}+1}\cdots d_{m}
, and
\beta_{\zeta}=\sup_{|x|\leq\zeta}|p(x)(1-p(x))|/(p^{\prime}(x))^{2}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
5
hold and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}
. There exist absolute constants
C_{1,m},C_{2,m},C_{3,m}>0
such that if

(a)

Sparsity of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
:
