|\Omega^{*}|\leq\min\bigg\{\frac{d^{*}r}{\min(d_{1}^{*},d_{2}^{*})},\ C_{1,m}\zeta^{-2}\bar{\lambda}^{2}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{4}\bar{r}^{-1},\kappa_{0}^{-4m}\bar{r}^{-1}\big\}\bigg\}
,

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:
\underline{\lambda}^{2}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{4}\bar{r}^{-1},\kappa_{0}^{-4m}\bar{r}^{-1}\big\}\geq C_{2,m}\zeta L_{\zeta}\beta_{\zeta}[r(d_{1}^{\ast}+d_{2}^{\ast})d^{\ast}]^{1/2}
,

the output of Algorithm
7
satisfies the initialization in Theorem
5.7
with probability at least
1-C_{3,m}(d^{\ast})^{-1}
.

Compared with Theorem
5.7
, the required sparsity of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
and signal-to-noise ratio are more stringent to guarantee a warm initialization. It is typical that, oftentimes, the signal-to-noise ratio condition required by warm initialization is the primary bottleneck in tensor-related problems. See, for instance,
Xia et al. 2021
;
Zhang and Xia 2018
.

5.4
Poisson Tensor Robust PCA

In this section, we consider the Poisson tensor RPCA model. Suppose we observe
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
that satisfies

\forall\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}],~[\bm{\mathcal{Y}}]_{\omega}\sim\text{Poisson}(I\exp([\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}]_{\omega}+[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}))\text{~independently},

where
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\in(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{r},\mu_{1}},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha})
are the low rank part and sparse part respectively and
I>0
is the intensity parameter that is revealed as in
Han et al. 2020
. We choose the loss function to be the negative log-likelihood with scaling

\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}})=\frac{1}{I}\sum_{\omega}\left(-[\bm{\mathcal{Y}}]_{\omega}[\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega}+I\exp([\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}]_{\omega})\right).

This is an entry-wise loss, and simple calculation shows Assumptions
2
and
3
are satisfied with
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{*}=\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{*}=\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}:\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta,\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}},\bm{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\gamma\alpha}\}
with
b_{l,\zeta}=e^{-\zeta},b_{u,\zeta}=e^{\zeta}
. Since the parameter will become trivial in an unbounded set, we impose the following assumption which implies
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\frac{\zeta}{2}
and thus
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\zeta
.

Assumption 6
.

There exists a small
\zeta>0
such that
\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\zeta}{2}
,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
satisfies Assumption
1
with its largest singular value
\bar{\lambda}\leq c_{m}(\kappa_{0}\mu_{1})^{-m}\sqrt{\frac{d^{*}}{r^{*}}}\zeta
where
d^{*}=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
and
r^{*}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
.

Similar with the binary case, we also need to show
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}\|_{\ell_{\infty}},\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=O(\zeta)
. These are guaranteed by choosing
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=C_{1}\zeta
for some
C_{1}>1
depending only on
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1},m
and from Lemma
5.6
, when
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1},m=O(1)
and
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
, we have
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=O(\zeta)
. We summarize the result in the following Theorem.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\gamma>1,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}:=C_{1}\zeta
be the parameters used in Algorithm
2
for a constant
C_{1}>1
depending only on
\kappa_{0}\mu_{1}
and
m
via Lemma
5.6
.
Suppose Assumptions
1
and
6
hold. Assume
|\Omega^{\ast}|\asymp\alpha d^{\ast}
,
e^{2\zeta^{\prime}}\leq 0.4(\sqrt{\delta})^{-1}
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\zeta^{\prime}=(2C_{1}+1)\zeta
, and

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\big\}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq c_{2,m}\zeta

and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:

\displaystyle\underline{\lambda}\cdot\min\big\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\bar{r}^{1/2})^{-1}\big\}\geq C_{2,m}\Big(\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|\frac{1+e^{\zeta^{\prime}}}{e^{-\zeta^{\prime}}}\cdot e^{\zeta}+\sqrt{(r^{*}+\bar{d}\bar{r})e^{\zeta}/I}\Big),\text{~and~}I\geq Ce^{\zeta}\log(d^{*})

(c)

Sparsity condition:
\alpha\leq c_{3,m}e^{-8\zeta^{\prime}}(\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m})^{-1}
and
\gamma\geq 1+(4m)^{-1}\cdot e^{8\zeta^{\prime}}

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m},c_{3,m},C_{2,m}>0
are some constants depending on
m
only. If the stepsize
\beta\in[0.005e^{-3\zeta^{\prime}},0.36e^{-3\zeta^{\prime}}]
, after
l_{\max}
iterations, with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{*}}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{l_{\max}}\cdot\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{1,\delta}\frac{r^{*}+\bar{d}\bar{r}}{I/e^{\zeta}}+C_{3}e^{2\zeta}\cdot\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{4\zeta^{\prime}}\big(C_{4,m}\alpha\bar{r}^{m}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}+C_{5,m}(\gamma-1)^{-1}\big)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{6,m}e^{2\zeta+2\zeta^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|

where
C_{3}>0
depends only on
\delta,\zeta,m
, and
C_{4,m},C_{5,m},C_{6,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
. Moreover, if
l_{\max}
is chosen large enough such that the second term on RHS of (
5.9
) dominates and assume
\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{1}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}(\bar{r}\bar{d}+r^{\ast})\lesssim_{m}O(\underline{d}^{m-1})
, we get with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{*}}
that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{6}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}(\bar{r}^{m}/\underline{d}^{m-1})^{1/2}\Big(\frac{r^{*}+\bar{d}\bar{r}}{I}+\gamma|\Omega^{*}|\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{7}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\bar{r}^{m/2}/\underline{d}^{(m-1)/2}\cdot\Big(\sqrt{(r^{*}+\bar{d}\bar{r})/I}+|\Omega^{*}|^{1/2}\Big)+C_{8}

where
C_{6},C_{7},C_{8}>0
depend only on
\gamma,\delta,\zeta,m
.

From Theorem
5.9
, after a properly chosen
l_{\max}
iterations, we will obtain
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O(\frac{r^{*}+\bar{d}\bar{r}}{I/e^{\zeta}}+e^{2\zeta}\cdot\gamma|\Omega^{\ast}|)
.
As a special case when
|\Omega^{*}|=0
, our result matches the previous result in Poisson tensor PCA in
Han et al. 2020
that is rate optimal under the same requirements on the intensity parameter
I
. When there are outliers, the error for the estimation of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
is further influenced by the outliers.

Initialization.
We shall adopt the initialization proposed in
Han et al. 2020
with slight modification. The theoretical guarantee is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
6
hold. There exist absolute constants
c,C>0
such that if
I\geq C\max\{\bar{d},\underline{\lambda}^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(d_{i}r_{i}+d_{i}^{-}r_{i})\bar{r}\}
, and the sparsity of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
satisfies
|\Omega^{*}|\leq c\zeta^{-2}\underline{\lambda}^{2}\bar{r}^{-1}
, then
the output of Algorithm
4
satisfies the initialization requirement in Theorem
5.9
with probability at least
1-1/d^{*}
.

Algorithm 4

Initialization for Poisson RPCA

Set
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\log(\frac{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}+1/2}{I})
.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}=\mathscr{H}^{{\rm HO}}_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
.

Return
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}=\textsf{Trim}_{\eta,\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0})
with
\eta=16\mu_{1}\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\|_{\rm F}/(7\sqrt{d^{*}})
.

6
When Sparse Component is Absent

In this section, we consider the special case when the sparse component is absent, i.e.,
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}={\bf 0}
. For the exact low-rank tensor model, we observe that many conditions in Section
4
can be relaxed. A major difference is that the spikiness condition is generally not required for exact low-rank model. Consequently, the trimming step in Algorithm
2
is unnecessary. Therefore, it suffices to simply apply the Riemannian gradient descent algorithm to solve for the underlying low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
. For ease of exposition, the procedure is summarized in Algorithm
5
(largely the same as Algorithm
2
).

Algorithm 5

Riemannian Gradient Descent for Exact Low-rank Estimate

Initialization:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}

\in

\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}
and stepsize
\beta>0

for

l=0,1,\cdots,l_{\max}

do

\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}=\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})

\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l+1}=\mathscr{H}^{{\rm HO}}_{\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l})

end

for

Output:

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}

Algorithm
5
runs fast and guarantees favourable convergence performances under weaker conditions than Theorem
4.1
. Indeed, since there is no sparse component, only Assumption
2
is required to guarantee the convergence of Algorithm
5
.
Similarly as Section
4
, the error of final estimate produced by Algorithm
5
is characterized by the gradient at
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
. With a slightly abuse of notation, denote

\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}=\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{M}_{2\mathbf{r}},\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\rm F}\leq 1}\langle\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}),\bm{\mathcal{X}}\rangle.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose Assumption
2
holds with
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}={\bf 0}
and
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{\ast}=\{\bm{\mathcal{T}}:\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{0,m}\underline{\lambda},\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathbf{r}}\}
for a small constant
c_{0,m}>0
depending on
m
only, also suppose
1.5b_{l}b_{u}^{-2}\leq 1
and
0.75b_{l}b_{u}^{-1}\geq\delta^{1/2}
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and the stepsize
\beta\in[0.4b_{l}b_{u}^{-2},1.5b_{l}b_{u}^{-2}]
in Algorithm
5
. Assume

(a)

Initialization:
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}\cdot c_{1,m}\delta\bar{r}^{-1/2}

(b)

Signal-to-noise ratio:
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}/\underline{\lambda}\leq c_{2,m}\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2}

where
c_{1,m},c_{2,m}>0
are small constants depending only on
m
.
Then for all
l=1,\cdots,l_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{\delta}\textsf{Err}_{2\bm{r}}^{2}

where
C_{\delta}>0
is a constant depending only on
\delta
. Then after at most
l_{\max}\asymp\log\big(\underline{\lambda}/\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}\big)
iterations (also depends on
b_{l},b_{u},m,\bar{r}
and
\beta
), we get

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq C\cdot\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}},

where the constant
C>0
depends on only
b_{l},b_{u},m,\bar{r}
and
\beta
.

Note that Theorem
6.1
holds without spikiness condition in contrast with Theorem
4.1
.
It makes sense for the model has no missing values or sparse corruptions. The assumptions on loss function are also weaker (e.g., no need to be an entry-wise loss or entry-wisely smooth) than those in Theorem
4.1
. As a result, Theorem
6.1
is also applicable to the low-rank tensor regression model among others. See
(
Han et al. 2020
;
Chen et al. 2019
;
Xia et al. 2020
)
and references therein.
The initialization and signal-to-noise conditions are similar to those in Theorem
4.1
, e.g., by setting
|\Omega^{\ast}|=\alpha=0
there. In addition, the error of final estimate depends only on
\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}
. Interestingly, the contraction rate does not depend on the condition number
\kappa_{0}
.

Comparison with existing literature

In
(
Han et al. 2020
)
, the authors proposed a general framework for exact low-rank tensor estimation based on regularized jointly gradient descent on the core tensor and associated low-rank factors. Their method is fast and achieves statistical optimality in various models. In contrast, our algorithm is based on Riemannian gradient descent, requires no regularization and also runs fast. An iterative tensor projection algorithm was studied in
(
Yu and Liu 2016
)
. But their method only applies to tensor regression. Other notable works focusing only on tensor regression include
(
Zhang et al. 2020a
;
Zhou et al. 2013
;
Hao et al. 2020
;
Sun et al. 2017
;
Li et al. 2018
;
Pan et al. 2018
)
.
A general projected gradient descent algorithm was proposed in
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
for generalized low-rank tensor estimation. For Tucker low-rank tensors, their algorithm is similar to our Algorithm
5
except that they use vanilla gradient
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
while we use the Riemannian gradient
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l}
. As explained in Section
3
, using the vanilla gradient can cause heavy computation burdens in the subsequent steps.
Riemannian gradient descent algorithm for tensor completion was initially proposed by
(
Kressner et al. 2014
)
. They focused only on tensor completion model and did not investigate its theoretical guarantees.
Recently in
(
Cai et al. 2020
)
, the Riemannian gradient descent algorithm is applied for noiseless tensor regression and its convergence analysis is proved.

7
Numerical Comparisons with Existing Methods

We test the performances of our algorithms on synthetic datasets, specifically for the four applications studied in Section
5
. Due to page limit, here we only present the comparative simulation results with competing methods on SG-RPCA and binary tensor learning. The comprehensive simulation results and the performance of proposed BIC-type criterion are collected in the supplementary file.

Choice of parameters
.
First of all, for the stepsize, we choose
\beta
that lies in the range we provide in the theories. For the spikiness parameter
\mu_{1}
, our theorem only requires it to be larger than the truth, we can initially set
\mu_{1}=2^{m}+\log(\bar{d})
and gradually increase it by a factor of
2
if the algorithm fails to converge. For the rank
\mathbf{r}
and sparsity
\alpha
, we treat them as given or select them by the BIC-type criterion (
3.3
). Note that
\gamma

only
plays a role in the term
\gamma\alpha=:\alpha^{\prime}
, i.e., the desired sparsity level. If the true
\alpha
is unknown, then the BIC-type criterion actually searches for
\alpha^{\prime}
in which case
\gamma
is irrelevant and we simply set it to
1
; if the true
\alpha
is known, then we initially set
\gamma=1.1
or
2
and gradually increase it by a factor of
1.5
if the algorithm fails to converge.
In the case of SG-RPCA and tensor PCA with heavy tailed noise,
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=\infty
; and in the case of binary tensor, we follow the choice in
Wang and Li 2020
and set
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=1
.

For the first experiment, we compare RGrad and PGD
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
on noisy tensor decomposition without outliers, i.e., no
\alpha
or
\gamma
. The low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=300
and Tucker rank
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\top}
is generated from the HOSVD of a trimmed standard normal tensor. The noise tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
has i.i.d. entries sampled from
{\rm N}(0,\sigma_{z}^{2})
. Both algorithm terminate either when the relative error
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}\|_{\rm F}/\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}\|_{\rm F}<0.001
or the maximum iteration (100) is reached.
The noise level
\sigma_{z}
ranges from 0.01 to 0.05. For each fixed
\sigma_{z}
, 10 random instances for both algorithms. are conducted. The result is displayed in the left plot of Figure
1(a)
. We see that the statistical performance of RGrad and PGD, when there is no outliers, are similar. However, the right panel of Figure
1(a)
shows that the per-step runtime using RGrad is only roughly 1/4 of the per-step runtime using PGD. This illustrates the computational efficiency of using RGrad over PGD.

For the second experiment, we set
d=100
and
\sigma_{z}=0.01
. Given a sparsity level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the entries of sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
are i.i.d. sampled from
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}\times{\rm Be}(\alpha)\times{\rm N}(0,1)
, which ensures
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{S}_{O(\alpha)}
with high probability. Here the constant
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}
is set as
0.1
or
1
modeling the two cases of small magnitude and large magnitude, respectively. The sparsity
\alpha
is varied between 0.025 and 0.1, and
\gamma=1.1
for RGrad. We refer to
Gu et al. 2014
’s method as convex relaxation and
Lu et al. 2016
’s method as tubal-tRPCA. The results are displayed in Figure
1(b)
. Here RGrad (BIC) means that
\alpha
is treated as unknown and selected by BIC-type criterion (
3.3
). It shows that the proposed BIC-type criterion works nicely in SG-RPCA. We can see the tubal-tRPCA performs poorly due to the ignorance of the low rank structure along the third direction. When the magnitude of the outliers is small, the performance of PGD and RGrad are similar. However, when the magnitude of the outliers is large, PGD performs poorly since it cannot deal with the outliers. The performance of convex relaxation is also worse than RGrad since it unfolds a tensor into an unbalanced matrix, and is statistically sub-optimal.

For the third experiment, we compare RGrad and PGD on binary tensor learning. Here
d=100
and
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\top}
. The incoherent
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
is generated such that
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\approx 5
. The entries of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
are i.i.d. sampled from
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}\times{\rm Be}(\alpha)
. Here the constant
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}
is set as
1
or
10
modeling the two cases of small magnitude and large magnitude, respectively. The sparsity
\alpha
is varied between
0.005
and
0.02
, and
\gamma=1.1
for RGrad. Initialization is obtained by the algorithm in the supplement. The link function is set to
p(x)=(1+e^{-x/5})^{-1}
. Here RGrad (BIC) has a similar meaning as above.
The results are displayed in Figure
1(c)
. When the magnitude of outliers is small (
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}=1
), the performance of RGrad and PGD are comparable. However, when the magnitude of the outliers become large (
\textsf{S}_{\textsf{amp}}=10
), PGD cannot handle them well while our proposed algorithm has a much better performance.

.

.

8
Real Data: International Commodity Trade Flows

We collected the international commodity trade data from the API provided by UN website
https://comtrade.un.org
. The dataset contains the monthly information of imported commodities by countries from Jan. 2010 to Dec. 2016 (
84
months in total). For simplicity, we focus on
50
countries among which
35
are from Europe,
9
from America,
5
from Asia
2
2

2

Egypt is at the cross of Eastern Africa and Western Asia. For simplicity, we treat it as an Asian country. In addition, Turkey is treated as an Eastern European country rather than a Western Asian country.
and
1
from Africa. All the commodities are classified into
100
categories based on the
2
-digit HS code (
https://www.foreign-trade.com/reference/hscode.htm
). Thus, the raw data is a
50\times 50\times 100\times 84
tensor. At any month and for any category of commodity, there is a directed and weighted graph of size
50\times 50
depicting the trade flow between countries. The international trade has cyclic pattern annually. Since we are less interested in the time domain, we eliminate the fourth dimension by simply adding up the entries. Finally, we end up with a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
of size
50\times 50\times 100
.

In Figure
2
, circular plots are presented for illustrating the special trade patterns of some commodities. The countries are grouped and coloured by continent, i.e., Europe by red, Asia by green, America by blue and Africa by black. The links represent the directional trade flow between nations and are coloured based on the starting end of the link. The position of starting end of the link is shorter than the other end to give users the feeling that the link is moving out. The thickness of link indicates the volume of trade. From the top-left plot, we observe that Japan imports a large volume of tobacco related commodities; Germany is the largest exporter; Poland and Brazil are the second and third largest exporter; USA both import and export a large quantity of tobacco commodity. The top-right plot shows that USA and Canada import and export large volumes of mineral fuels; Malaysia exports lots of mineral fuels to Japan; Algeria exports a large quantity of miner fuels which plays the major role of international trade of this Africa country. The middle-left plot shows that Portugal is the largest exporter of Cork, and European countries are the major exporter and importer of this commodity. From the middle-right plot, we observe that Pakistan is the major exporter of Cotton in Asia; the European countries Turkey, Italy and Germany all export and import large volumes of cotton; USA exports a great deal of cotton to Mexico,Turkey and Philippines. The bottom-left plot shows that Malaysia and Belgium are the largest exporter of Tin and USA is the major importer. Finally, the bottom-right plot shows that Switzerland is the single largest exporter of clocks and watches; USA is the major importer; France and Germany both export and import large quantities of clocks and watches.

Figure 2
:
International Commodity Trade Flow. Import and export flow of some commodities.

We implement the SG-RPCA framework as Section
5.1
to analyze the tensor
\log(1+\bm{\mathcal{A}})
, where a logarithmic transformation helps shrink the extremely large entries.
The Tucker ranks are set as
(3,3,3)
, although most of the results seem insensitive to the ranks so long as they are bounded by
5
(BIC values within a
1\%
discrepancy). We apply the BIC-type criterion (
3.3
) and the detailed BIC result is postponed to the supplement. It suggests that any
\alpha
between
0.002
and
0.03
yield similar (within 0.1% discrepancy) BIC values. See also top left and right panel in Figure
3
. The algorithm is initialized by a HOSVD, which finally produces a low-rank
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
and a sparse
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
. We shall use
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
to uncover underlying relations among countries, and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
to examine distinctive trading patterns of certain commodities.

In particular, the singular vectors of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
are utilized to illustrate the community structure of nations. Note that the
1
st-dim and
2
nd-dim singular vectors of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
are distinct because the trading flows are directed. We observe that the
2
nd-dim singular vectors often render better results. Then, a procedure of multi-dimensional scaling is adopted to visualize the rows of these singular vectors. We note that, though the BIC-type criterion suggests an
\alpha\in[0.002,0.03]
, intriguing phenomenons are observed for larger values of
\alpha
.
The results are presented in Figure
3
for four choices of
\alpha\in\{0.003,0.03,0.1,0.3\}
. All the plots in Figure
3
reveal certain degrees of the geographical relations among countries. It is reasonable since regional trade partnerships generally dominate the inter-continental trade relations. The European countries (coloured in blue) are mostly separated from the others. Overall, countries from America (coloured in red) and Asia (coloured in magenta) are less separable especially when
\alpha
is large. For small
\alpha
like
0.003
or
0.03
, the
5
Asian countries are clustered together and the major
8
American nations lie on the top-right corner of the plot. The two geographically close African countries Algeria and Egypt are also placed together in the top-left plot of Figure
3
, as is the case with the Western European nations such as United Kingdom, Spain, France, Germany and Italy.

Figure
3
show that the low-rank estimate is sensitive to the larger sparsity ratio. Interesting shifts appear as
\alpha
increases. Indeed, the geographical relations become a less important factor but the economic similarity plays the dominating role.
For instance, some Asian and American nations split and merge into two clusters. The three large economies US, Canada and Japan are merged into one cluster, while the other small and less-developed Asian and American countries are merged into another cluster. It may be caused by that these three large economies are better at advanced technology and share similar structures in exporting high end commodities. Moreover, as
\alpha
increases, the African country Algeria moves closer to the less-developed American and Asian nations. All these nations including Algeria rely heavily on exporting natural resources even if Algeria is geographically far from the others. Another significant shift is that the European countries split into two clusters as
\alpha
increases. Moreover, one cluster comprising those wealthy and advanced Western European countries move closer to the group of US, Japan and Canada. These countries have close ties in trading high end products and components, although they belong to distinct continents. The other cluster includes mostly the Central and Eastern European countries, among which regional trade flows are particularly intense. Interestingly, there are two outlier countries Ireland (north-western Europe) and Antigua and Barbuda (a small island country in middle America). They do not merge into any clusters. The magnitudes of coordinates of these two points suggest that their international trade is not active.

(a)

Sparsity level: top-left,
\alpha=0.003
; top-right,
\alpha=0.03
; bottom-left,
\alpha=0.1
; bottom-right,
\alpha=0.3
.

Figure 3
:
Visualize the relations between countries by spectral estimates. Tucker ranks are set as
(3,3,3)
and the low-rank tensor is estimated by SG-RPCA as in Section
5.1
with HOSVD initialization. Blue coloured countries are from Europe, red from America, magenta from Asia and black from Africa. When
\alpha
is small, clusters have strong implications on the geographical closeness between nations; As
\alpha
becomes large, economic structures become the major factor in that large and advanced economies tend to merge, and so do low end economies and natural resource reliant economies. But our BIC-type criterion suggests to choose
\alpha\in[0.002,0.03]
.

We now look into the slices of the sparse estimate
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
and investigate the distinctive trading patterns of certain commodities. As the sparsity ratio
\alpha
grows, the slices of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
become denser whose patterns are more difficult to examine. For better exposition, we mainly focus on small values of
\alpha
like
0.03
. The results are presented in Figure
4
. The top-left plot shows that Algeria exports exceptionally large volumes of mineral fuels that can not be explained by the low-rank tensor estimate. Similarly, based on the top-right plot, we observe that the exact low-rank tensor PCA fails to explain the trading export of cork by Portugal. Fortunately, these interesting and significant trading patterns can be easily captured by the additional sparse tensor estimate. The bottom-left and bottom-right plots of Figure
4
showcase the unusual exports of cotton by Pakistan and exports of Tin by Malaysia, respectively. These findings echo some trading patterns displayed in Figure
2
.

(a)

Commodity: top-left, Mineral Fuels; top-right, Cork; bottom-left, Cotton; bottom-right, Tin.

Figure 4
:
Heatmaps of the slices of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
. They reveal distinctive trading patterns of certain commodities. The sparsity ratio is set at
\alpha=0.03
.

We now compare
Gu et al. 2014
’s method (convex relaxation) and
Lu et al. 2016
’s method (tubal-tRPCA) with our method in terms of prediction error on the international trade flow dataset. As in Section
8
, we analyze the tensor
\log(1+\bm{\mathcal{A}})
. We split
\log(1+\bm{\mathcal{A}})
into two parts, namely
\log(1+\bm{\mathcal{A}})=:\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{train}}+\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{test}}
, where
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{test}}
is generated by randomly taking 10% of the non-zero entries of
\log(1+\bm{\mathcal{A}})
. We then apply tubal-tRPCA with the default parameter the authors provide
3
3

3

Their codes are available at https://github.com/canyilu/tensor-completion-under-linear-transform.
and convex relaxation with carefully tuned parameters
4
4

4

The codes in
Gu et al. 2014
is not publicly released so we have to tune the parameters by ourselves.
. We use the proposed BIC-type criterion to select the rank and sparsity. As the left panel of Figure
5
suggests, we choose
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
, and the right panel of Figure
5
shows the BIC is less sensitive to
\alpha
for a small range.

Figure 5
:
BIC values on the International Trade Flow Data. Left: BIC values for different rank and sparsity; Right: Zoom in on the case
r=3
.

Therefore we set the rank as
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
and try
\alpha=0.01,0.02,0.03
. The error is measured in terms of the test error
\|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}]_{\Omega_{\text{test}}}-\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{test}}\|_{\rm F}
and the results are presented in Table

.

in terms of prediction error on the international trade flow data. Our BIC criterion suggests any

When
\alpha=0
, all methods perform poorly because the existence of outliers distort the low-rank estimate making it ineffective in prediction. Meanwhile, if
\alpha
is too large, say
0.1
, the sparse component might incorrectly absorb useful information from the low-rank component which, as a result, sabotages its prediction accuracy. Fortunately, our method with the BIC suggested
\alpha
indeed significantly outperforms other methods.

SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL for “Generalized Low-rank plus Sparse Tensor Estimation by Fast Riemannian Optimization”

9
Higher Order Orthogonal Iteration Algorithm

The HOOI algorithm is summarized as follows which is applied for the initialization in Section
5.1
and
5.2
.

Algorithm 6

HOOI

Input:
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}},\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{m})
, maximum number of iteration:
t_{\max}
.

Let
t=0
, initiate
\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{0}=\textrm{SVD}_{r_{i}}(\mathcal{M}_{i}(\bm{\mathcal{Y}}))
,
i\in[m]
.

for

t=1,\ldots,t_{\max}

do

for

i=1,\ldots,m

do

\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{t}=\textrm{SVD}_{r_{i}}\big(\mathcal{M}_{i}(\bm{\mathcal{Y}})(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{m}^{t-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i+1}^{t-1}\otimes\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i-1}^{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{1}^{t-1})\big)

end

for

end

for

Output:
\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}=\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{t_{max}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\times_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}\widehat{}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{T}
.

10
More Numerical Simulations

In Section
10.1
, we apply the proposed BIC-type criterion for SG-RPCA and binary tensor learning and demonstrate its effectiveness on synthetic data. Through Section
10.2
-
10.5
, we treat
\mathbf{r}
and
\alpha
as given and test the performance of our estimator with respect to different choices of
\gamma
. Other algorithmic parameters like
\mu_{1}
and
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}
are decided as explained in Section
7
.

10.1
Performance of BIC-type Criterion

We test the performance of BIC-type criterion (
3.3
) for SG-RPCA and binary tensor learning. As explained in Section
7
,
\gamma
is set to
1
and
\mu_{1}=2^{m}+\log(\bar{d})
.
More exactly, the BIC-type criterion for SG-RPCA (assuming Gaussian noise with equal but unknown variances) is

{\rm BIC}(\mathbf{r},\alpha):=\big(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}\|_{\ell_{0}}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}d_{i}\big)\cdot\log(d^{\ast})+d^{\ast}\log(\|\bm{\mathcal{A}}-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r},\alpha}\|_{\rm F}^{2}).

The true tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=100
and
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
. We test two true sparsity levels
\alpha\in\{0.05,0.1\}
.
The true tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
satisfies
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=0.1
, and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
is generated as above satisfying
\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=4
, and all entries of
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}^{*}
satisfy i.i.d.
{\rm N}(0,\sigma_{z}^{2})
with
\sigma_{z}=0.01
. For each
\alpha\in\{0.05,0.1\}
, we test, in our algorithm,
\mathbf{r}\in\{(1,1,1),(2,2,2),(3,3,3),(4,4,4),(5,5,5)\}
and
\alpha\in(0.02,0.2)
. The results are displayed in Figure
6
. The BIC-values are sensitive to both
\mathbf{r}
and
\alpha
. We note that the BIC-values for
\mathbf{r}>3
are strictly larger than that of
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)
, but the difference is too small to be spotted in the figures.

Figure 6
:
BIC values for SG-RPCA; the true rank
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
. Left: true
\alpha=0.05
; Right: true
\alpha=0.1
.

For robust binary tensor learning, we also set
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
and
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=100
, and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
is generated as above.
The true
\alpha\in\{0.005,0.01\}
. We fix
p(x)=(1+e^{-10x})^{-1}
.
The BIC criterion for the binary case is:

{\rm BIC}(\mathbf{r},\alpha):=\big(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}\|_{\ell_{0}}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}d_{i}\big)\cdot\log(d^{\ast})-2\sum_{\omega}\big([\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\log p([\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}]_{\omega})+\big(1-[\bm{\mathcal{A}}]_{\omega}\big)\log\big(1-p([\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}]_{\omega})\big)\big).

For each true
\alpha\in\{0.005,0.01\}
, we test BIC for
\mathbf{r}\in\{(1,1,1),(2,2,2),(3,3,3),(4,4,4),(5,5,5)\}
and
\alpha
varying from 0.001 to 0.015.
The results are displayed in Figure
7
showing that BIC is more sensitive to
\mathbf{r}
and less sensitive to
\alpha
for a small range. After the true
\mathbf{r}
is identified, the BIC criterion works reasonably well for selecting
\alpha
.

Figure 7
:
BIC values for binary tensor learning; the true rank
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
. Left: true
\alpha=0.005
; Right: true
\alpha=0.01
. The BIC curve for
\mathbf{r}=(3,3,3)^{\top}
is highlighted.

10.2
Tensor Sub-Gaussian Robust PCA

The low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=100
and Tucker ranks
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\top}
is generated from the HOSVD of a trimmed standard normal tensor. It satisfies the spikiness condition, with high probability, and has singular values
\bar{\lambda}\approx 3
and
\underline{\lambda}\approx 1
. Given a sparsity level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the entries of sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
are i.i.d. sampled from
{\rm Be}(\alpha)\times{\rm N}(0,1)
, which ensures
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{S}_{O(\alpha)}
with high probability. This ensures that the non-zero entries of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
have typically much larger magnitudes than the entries of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}
.
The noise tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
has i.i.d. entries sampled from
{\rm N}(0,\sigma_{z}^{2})
. The default choice of
\gamma
is 2,
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=\infty
and
\mu_{1}
is set as previously. The convergence performances of
\log(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}/\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F})
by Algorithm
2
are examined and presented in the left panels of Figure
8
.

The top-left plot in Figure
8
displays the effects of
\alpha
on the convergence of Algorithm
2
. It shows that the convergence speed of Algorithm
2
is insensitive to
\alpha
, while the error of final estimates
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
is related to
\alpha
. This is consistent with the claims of Theorem
5.1
. In the middle-left plot of Figure
8
, we observe that, for a fixed sparsity level
\alpha
, the error of final estimates grows as the tuning parameter
\gamma
becomes larger. The bottom-left plot of Figure
8
shows the convergence of Algorithm
2
for different noise levels. All these plots confirm the fast convergence of our Riemannian gradient descent algorithm. In particular, there are stages during which the log relative error decreases linearly w.r.t. the number of iterations, as proved in Theorem
4.1
.

The statistical stability of the final estimates by Algorithm
2
is demonstrated in the right panels of Figure
8
. Each curve represents the average relative error of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
based on
10
simulations, and the error bar shows the confidence region by one empirical standard deviation. Based on these plots, we observe that the standard deviations of
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
grow as the noise level
\sigma_{z}
, the sparsity level
\alpha
or the tuning parameter
\gamma
increases.

10.3
Tensor PCA with Heavy-tailed Noise

The low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=100
and Tucker ranks
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\top}
is generated from the HOSVD of a trimmed standard normal tensor, as in Section
10.2
. Given a parameter
\theta
, we generate the noisy tensor whose entries are i.i.d. and satisfy the Student-t distribution with degree of freedom
\theta
. But notice here we also apply a global scaling to better control the noise standard deviation. We denote the noisy tensor after scaling by
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
. This generated tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}
satisfies Assumption
4
with the same parameter
\theta
. Once the parameter
\theta
and global scaling are given, we are able to calculate the variance
\sigma_{z}^{2}
. The convergence performances of
\log(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}/\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F})
by Algorithm
2
are examined and presented in the upper panels of Figure
9
.

In this experiment, we set
\gamma=2,\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=\infty
and
\mu_{1}
as previously. The top-left plot in Figure
9
displays the effects of
\alpha
on the convergence of Algorithm
2
. The case
\alpha=0
reduces to the normal Riemannian gradient descent, which cannot output a satisfiable result due to the heavy-tailed noise, even if a warm initialization is provided. This shows the importance of gradient pruning in Algorithm
2
. When
\alpha>0
, the convergence speed of the algorithm is insensitive to
\alpha
, but the final estimates
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
is related to
\alpha
. In the top-right plot of Figure
9
, we observe the error becomes larger as
\theta
decreases (or equivalently, as
\sigma_{z}^{2}
increases). All these results match the claim of Theorem
5.4
and confirm the fast convergence of Riemannian gradient descent. And there are indeed stages where the log relative error decreases linearly w.r.t. the number of iterations.

The statistical stability of the final estimates by Algorithm
2
applied to tensor PCA with heavy-tailed noise is demonstrated in the bottom panel of Figure
9
. Each curve represents the average relative error of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
based on
5
simulations, and the error bar shows the confidence region by one empirical standard deviation. Based on these plots, we observe that for each fixed
\theta
(or
\sigma_{z}^{2}
, equivalently), we need to choose
\alpha
carefully to achieve the best performance. This is reasonable since in the heavy-tail noise setting, we do not know the sparsity of outliers. Also, the figure shows that Algorithm
2
is stable for different
\alpha
and
\theta
.

,

; Right: Change of

,

(b)

10.4
Binary Tensor Learning

In the binary tensor setting, we generate the low-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=100
and Tucker ranks
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\top}
from the HOSVD of a trimmed standard normal tensor. But here we did a scaling to
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
so that the singular value
\bar{\lambda}\approx 300
and
\underline{\lambda}\approx 100
. Given a sparsity level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the entries of sparse tensor
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
are i.i.d. sampled from
{\rm Be}(\alpha)\times{\rm N}(0,1)
, which ensures
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{S}_{O(\alpha)}
with high probability. We generate the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
in this way in order to meet the requirements of Assumption
5
. In the following experiments, we are considering the logistic link function with the scaling parameter
\sigma
, i.e.,
p(x)=(1+e^{-x/\sigma})^{-1}
. The default choice of
\gamma
is
1.1
,
\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}=1
and
\mu_{1}
is set as previously. The convergence performances of
\log(\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}/\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F})
by Algorithm
2
are examined and presented in the top two panels of Figure
10
.

The top-left plot in Figure
10
shows the effect of
\alpha
on the convergence of Algorithm
2
. From the figure, it is clear that the error of final estimates
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
is related to
\alpha
. This again verifies the results in Theorem
5.7
. In the top-right plot in Figure
10
, we can see the error of the final estimates increases as the parameter
\gamma
becomes larger.
All these experiments show that Riemannian gradient descent converges fast and there are stages when the log relative error decreases linearly w.r.t. the number of iterations.

The statistical stability of the final estimates by Algorithm
2
is demonstrated in the bottom panel of Figure
10
. Each curve represents the average relative error of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}
based on
5
simulations, and the error bar shows the confidence region by one empirical standard deviation. From these plots, we observe that the standard deviations of
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
grow as the noise level, the sparsity level
\alpha
or the tuning parameter
\gamma
increases.

\zeta
and

.

I=10

. In the second experiment, for different values of

.

11
Real Data: Statisticians Hypergraph Co-authorship Network

This dataset
[
Ji and Jin 2016
]
contains the co-authorship relations of
3607
statisticians based on
3248
papers published in four prestigious statistics journals during 2003-2012. The co-authorship network thus has
3607
nodes and two nodes are connected by an edge if they collaborated on at least one paper. A giant connected component of this network consisting of
236
nodes is seen to be the “High-Dimensional Data Analysis” community. They also carried out community detection analysis to discover substructures in this giant component. See more details in
[
Ji and Jin 2016
]
.

We analyze the substructures of the giant component by treating it as a hypergraph co-authorship network. These
236
statisticians co-authored
542
papers
5
5

5

There are
328
single-authored papers. They provide no information to co-authorship relations, and are left out in our analysis.
, among which
356
papers have two co-authors,
162
papers have three co-authors and
24
papers have four co-authors. A
3
-uniform hypergraph co-authorship network is constructed by, for
i\neq j\neq k
, adding the hyperedge
(i,j,k)
if the authors
i,j,k
co-authored at least one paper, and adding the hyperedges
(i,i,j)
and
(i,j,j)
if the authors
i,j
co-authored at least one paper. The hyperedges are
undirected
resulting into a symmetric adjacency tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
. We adopt the framework from Section
5.1
to learn the latent low-rank tensor
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
in
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
, which is used to detect communities in the giant component. We emphasize that our primary goal is to present the new findings by taking into consideration of higher-order interactions among co-authors and applying novel robust tensor methods. It is not our intention to label an author with a certain community.

The Tucker ranks are set as
(4,4,4)
and sparsity ratio
\alpha
is varied at
\{0,10^{-4},5\times 10^{-4}\}
. The number of communities is set at
K=3
and the algorithm is initialized by the HOSVD of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
. To uncover community structures, we apply spectral clustering to the singular vectors of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
. The node degrees are severely heterogeneous with Peter Hall, Jianqing Fan and Raymond Carroll being the top-
3
statisticians in terms of
\#
of co-authors. The naive spectral clustering often performs poorly in the existence of heterogeneity, skewing to the high-degree nodes. Indeed, the top-left plot in Figure
12
shows that the naive spectral clustering identifies these three statisticians as the corners in a triangle, and puts Peter Hall in a single community. To mitigate the influence of node heterogeneity, we apply SCORE
[
Jin 2015
]
for community detection, which uses the leading singular vector of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
as normalization.

(a)

Top-left:
\alpha=10^{-4}
and naive spectral clustering; top-right:
\alpha=0
and SCORE; bottom-left:
\alpha=10^{-4}
and SCORE; bottom-right:
\alpha=5\times 10^{-4}
and SCORE.

Figure 12
:
Sub-structures detected in the “High-Dimensional Data Analysis” community based on the hypergraph co-authorship network. The Tucker ranks are set as
(4,4,4)
with varied sparsity ratio at
\{0,10^{-4},5\times 10^{-4}\}
and the algorithm is initialized by the HOSVD of adjacency tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
.

The community structures found by SCORE are displayed in Figure
12
. The top-right plot shows the three clusters identified by SCORE when the sparsity ratio is zero. The three communities are: 1). ‘‘North Carolina” group including researchers from Duke University, University of North Carolina and North Carolina State University, together with their close collaborators such as Debajyoti Sinha, Qi-Man Shao, Bing-Yi Jing, Michael J Todd and etc.; 2). ‘‘Carroll-Hall” group including researchers in non-parametric and semi-parametric statistics, functional estimation and high-dimensional statistics, together with collaborators; 3). ‘‘Fan and Others” group
6
6

6

We name it the “Fan and Others” group simply because many researchers in this group are the co-authors of Jianqing Fan. It is not our intention to rank/label the authors.
including
primarily
the researchers collaborating closely with Jianqing Fan or his co-authors, and other researchers who do not
obviously
belong to the first two groups. We note that the fields of researchers in “Fan and Others” group are quite diverse, some of which overlap with those in “Carroll-Hall” group and “North Carolina” group.
However, unlike the results in
[
Ji and Jin 2016
]
, the top-right plot in Figure
12
does not cluster the “Fan and Others” group into either the “North Carolina” group or “Carroll-Hall” group.

We then set the sparsity ratio of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
by
\alpha=10^{-4}
. The communities identified by SCORE based on the singular vectors of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
are illustrated in the bottom-left plot of Figure
12
. Compared with the top-right plot (
\alpha=0
), the three communities displayed in the bottom-left plot largely remain the same. But the group memberships of some authors do change. Notably, Debajyoti Sinha and Michael J Todd move from the “North Carolina” group to “Fan and Others” group; Abel Rodriguez moves from the “Carroll-Hall” group to “North-Carolina” group; several authors (e.g. Daniela M Witten, Jacob Bien, Pan Wei, Chiung-Yu Huang, Debashis Paul, Zhezhen Jin, Lan Zhang and etc.) move from the “Fan and Others” group to “Carroll-Hall” group; Hsin-Cheng Huang moves from the “North Carolina” group to “Carroll-Hall” group; Rasmus Waggepetersen moves from the “Carroll-Hall” group to “Fan and Others” group. These changes of memberships suggest that these authors may not have strong ties to the “North Carolina”, “Carroll-Hall” group or be the co-authors of Jianqing Fan. It may be more reasonable that these authors constitute a separate group.

This indeed happens when the sparsity ratio
\alpha
increases to a certain level. The bottom-right plot of Figure
12
shows the clustering result of SCORE when
\alpha=5\times 10^{-4}
. Compared with the top-right (
\alpha=0
) and bottom-left (
\alpha=10^{-4}
) plots, the community structure has a significant change. Indeed, the “Fan and Others” group now splits into a “Fan” group including Jianqing Fan and his co-authors, and an “Others” group including the researchers who do not have obvious ties with “Fan” group.
Moreover, the “Fan” group merges into the “Carroll-Hall” group, which coincides with the clustering result of SCORE when applied onto the graph co-authorship network (Fig. 6 in
[
Ji and Jin 2016
]
). Consequently, we name the three communities in the top-right plot by the “North Carolina”, “Carroll-Fan-Hall” and “Others” group. Interestingly, many of the authors in the “Others” group are those whose memberships change when the sparsity ratio
\alpha
increases from
0
to
10^{-4}
. See the top-right and bottom-left plots of Figure
12
.
In addition, we observe that, as
\alpha
increases from
10^{-4}
to
5\times 10^{-4}
, Donglin Zeng and Dan Yu Lin in the “Fan and Others” group moves to “North Carolina” group. This might be more reasonable since they both work at the University of North Carolina.

12
Proofs of theorems

12.1
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We prove the theorem by induction on
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
and
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
alternatively. From the initialization condition we have
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\min\{\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\bar{r}}},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\sqrt{\bar{r}})^{-1}\}\cdot\underline{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent.

Step 1: Bounding
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
for all
l\geq 0
.

Suppose we have
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent and
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1,m}\min\{\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\bar{r}}},(\kappa_{0}^{2m}\sqrt{\bar{r}})^{-1}\}\cdot\underline{\lambda}
.

Now we estimate
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
. Denote
\Omega_{l}=\text{supp}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})
and
\Omega^{*}=\text{supp}(\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})
.
For
\forall\omega\in\Omega_{l}
, from the construction of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}
in Algorithm
1
, we have by the definition of
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|\leq\min\nolimits_{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}}\|\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.1)

From Assumption
3
, we get

\displaystyle|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})]_{\omega}-[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|\geq b_{l}|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|.

(12.2)

Note that to use (
12.2
), we shall verify the neighborhood condition.
From the upper bound of
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
we have
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
, and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent.
Therefore, from Lemma
13.7
, we have:

|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}]_{\omega}|^{2}\leq C_{1,m}\bar{r}^{m}\underline{d}^{-(m-1)}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}.

So we have

|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}]_{\omega}|\leq C_{1,m}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{r}^{m}}{\underline{d}^{m-1}}}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2m}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq C_{1,m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{r}^{m-1}}{\underline{d}^{m-1}}}\underline{\lambda},

where the last inequality is from the upper bound of
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
.
As a result, we have

\big|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}\big|\leq\big|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}\big|+|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}]_{\omega}|+|[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}|\leq C_{1,m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{r}^{m-1}}{\underline{d}^{m-1}}}\underline{\lambda}+\textsf{k}_{\textsf{pr}}+\|\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}.

Thus, both
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
belong to the ball
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{*}
and thus (
12.2
) holds.

As a result of (
12.1
) and (
12.2
), we get for any
\omega\in\Omega_{l}

b_{l}\big|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}\big|\leq\big|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast})]_{\omega}\big|+\textsf{Err}_{\infty}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}

\displaystyle(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{2|\Omega_{l}|}{b_{l}^{2}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))-{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))+{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{2|\Omega_{l}|}{b_{l}^{2}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))-{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{4}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{2|\Omega_{l}|}{b_{l}^{2}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{6|\Omega_{l}|}{b_{l}^{2}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2},

(12.3)

where the last inequality is due to
\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq|\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}
and Assumption
3
since
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{*}
.

From (
12.1
), Lemma
13.8
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\Omega_{l}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2,m}b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}\alpha\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{6|\Omega_{l}|}{b_{l}^{2}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.4)

here
C_{2,m}>0
is an absolute constant depending only on
m
.

For
\forall\omega=(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m})\in\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}
, we have
|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|=|[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|
. Since the loss function is entry-wise by Assumption
3
, we have
[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}=[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l})]_{\omega}
. Clearly,
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
and
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
both belong to
\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{*}
, by Assumption
3
we get

|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}-[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|\geq b_{l}|[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|.

Now we bound
|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|
as follows. For any
\omega\in\Omega^{\ast}\backslash\Omega_{l}
,

\displaystyle|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|

\displaystyle=|[\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}]_{\omega}|\leq\frac{1}{b_{l}}|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}-[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{b_{l}}\left(|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|+|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{b_{l}}\left(|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|+|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})-\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|+|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})]_{\omega}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{b_{l}}|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|+\frac{b_{u}}{b_{l}}|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}]_{\omega}|+\frac{1}{b_{l}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty},

where the last inequality is again due to Assumption
3
since
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{*}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{4b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\|{\cal P}_{\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{4}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{\ast}\backslash\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.5)

Since
\omega\in\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}
, we have

\displaystyle|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|\leq\max\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}|\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{i}}^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}))|^{(\gamma\alpha d_{i}^{-})}

(12.6)

Now since we have
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\in\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}|\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{i}}^{T}\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))|^{\left((\gamma-1)\alpha d_{i}^{-}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\Big|\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{i}}^{\top}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))-\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\right)\Big|^{\left((\gamma-1)\alpha d_{i}^{-}\right)}+\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.7)

Using AM-GM inequality, we have:

\displaystyle|[\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l})]_{\omega}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\Big\|\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{i}}^{\top}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))-\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\right)\Big\|_{\rm F}^{2}}{(\gamma-1)\alpha d_{i}^{-}}+2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\Big\|\mathbf{e}_{\omega_{i}}^{\top}\left(\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))-\mathcal{M}_{i}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}))\right)\Big\|_{\rm F}^{2}}{(\gamma-1)\alpha d_{i}^{-}}+2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.8)

Now for all fixed
i\in[m]
, for all
\omega_{i}\in[d_{i}]
,
\omega_{i}
appears at most
\alpha d_{i}^{-}
times since
\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}
is an
\alpha
-fraction set. This observation together with (
12.1
) lead to the following:

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}}(\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}))\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\|\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})-\nabla\mathfrak{L}(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}}{\gamma-1}+2|\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2mb_{u}^{2}}{\gamma-1}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+2|\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}.

(12.9)

Therefore together with (
12.5
) and (
12.1
) and Lemma
13.8
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4mb_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\frac{1}{\gamma-1}+C_{4,m}\frac{b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}\alpha\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{16}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\backslash\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.10)

where
C_{4,m}>0
are constants depending only on
m
.
Now we combine (
12.4
) and (
12.10
) and we get

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4mb_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\frac{1}{\gamma-1}+C_{5,m}(\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}\frac{b_{u}^{2}}{b_{l}^{2}}\alpha\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{\ast}\cup\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.11)

where
C_{5,m}>0
depending only on
m
and
C_{1}>0
an absolute constant.

Now if we choose
\alpha\leq(C_{5,m}\kappa_{0}^{4m}\mu_{0}^{4m}\bar{r}^{m}\frac{b_{u}^{4}}{b_{l}^{4}})^{-1}
and
\gamma-1\geq 4m\frac{b_{u}^{4}}{b_{l}^{4}}
for some sufficient large constants
C_{5,m}>0
depending only on
m
, then we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{b_{l}^{2}}{b_{u}^{2}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.12)

and

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\frac{b_{l}}{b_{u}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l}|}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.13)

In addition, from the upper bound of
\|\bm{\mathcal{T}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
, (
12.13
) implies that
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{0}\underline{\lambda}
for a small
c_{0}>0
.
This fact is helpful later since it implies that
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l}
belongs to the ball
\mathbb{B}_{2}^{*}
and thus activates the conditions in Assumption
2
.

Step 2: bounding
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
for all
l\geq 1
.

From previous step, we have verified

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq\frac{b_{l}}{b_{u}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l}|}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\leq c_{0}\underline{\lambda}.

(12.14)

And from the Algorithm
2
,
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}=\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta_{l},\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1})
.
Now from Lemma
13.6
, we get,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|\textsf{Trim}_{\zeta_{l},\mathbf{r}}(\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1})-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{m}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{r}}}{\underline{\lambda}}\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{3}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{4})\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta^{2})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+6\delta^{-1}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}+C_{1}\left(1+b_{u}+b_{u}^{2}\right)b_{l}^{-2}\left(|\Omega^{*}|+\gamma\alpha d^{*}\right)\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.15)

Notice to use Lemma
13.6
, we need to verify
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
, which we will check momentarily.
Also, from (
12.1
) and the signal-to-noise ration condition, we get

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq c_{1}\min\{\delta^{2}\bar{r}^{-1/2},\kappa_{0}^{-2m}\bar{r}^{-1/2}\}\cdot\underline{\lambda}.

On the other hand, from lemma
13.6
, we have
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}
is
(2\mu_{1}\kappa_{0})^{2}
-incoherent. Further, from Lemma
13.7
and the definition of
\textsf{k}_{\infty}
we have
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}\in\mathbb{B}_{\infty}^{\ast}
.
This finishes the induction for the error
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
. Now the only thing we need to check is the upper bound for
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
.

Step 2.1: bounding
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
.

From the Algorithm
2
, we have for arbitrary
1\geq\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{2})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}+(1+\frac{2}{\delta})\beta^{2}\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

(12.16)

Now we consider the bound for
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}-2\beta\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle~~~~+\beta^{2}\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

(12.17)

The upper bound of
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
ensures that
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}\in\mathbb{B}_{2}^{*}
.
Using the smoothness condition in Assumption
2
, we get

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

(12.18)

Now we consider the bound for
|\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle|
. First we have:

\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle=\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle-\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}^{\perp}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle.

The estimation of
\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle
is as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle-\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq b_{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}-\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle,

(12.19)

where the last inequality follows from Assumption
2
. And the estimation of
\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}^{\perp}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle
is as follows:

\displaystyle|\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}^{\perp}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}^{\perp}(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}\|\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1,m}b_{u}}{\underline{\lambda}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

(12.20)

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
13.1
. Together with (
12.1
) and (
12.1
), we get,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle\geq b_{l}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}-\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle~~~~-\frac{C_{1,m}b_{u}}{\underline{\lambda}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

(12.21)

Together with (
12.18
) and (
12.1
), we get

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2\beta b_{u}\frac{C_{1,m}}{\underline{\lambda}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~+(\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}-2\beta b_{l})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~+2\beta|\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*},\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\rangle|

(12.22)

In order to bound (
12.1
), we derive separately the bound for each terms.

Bounding
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}
.

From the bound for
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
in (
12.14
), we get,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+2\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.23)

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq 2\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.24)

Bounding
|\langle\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\rangle|
.

We first bound
\|\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
by (
12.24
):

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq b_{u}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}+\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq 2b_{u}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+\frac{C_{1}b_{u}}{b_{l}}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.25)

Now we estimate
|\langle\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\rangle|
from (
12.14
) and (
12.1
) as follows,

\displaystyle~~~~|\langle\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\rangle|\leq\|\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq(0.02b_{l}+0.01\beta b_{u}^{2})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}b_{u}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.26)

Bounding
|\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\rangle|
.

From (
12.14
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*},\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq(0.01\frac{b_{l}}{b_{u}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|}\textsf{Err}_{\infty})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq 0.02\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}}{b_{l}^{2}}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.27)

Now we go back to (
12.1
) and from (
12.1
) - (
12.1
), we get:

\displaystyle~~~~\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-1.84\beta b_{l}+5\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+C_{1}(1+b_{u}+b_{u}^{2})b_{l}^{-2}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.28)

where the condition
\underline{\lambda}\geq C_{1,m}\frac{b_{u}}{b_{l}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}
is used in the last step.

By combining (
12.1
) and (
12.1
), we get

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{2})\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}-\beta\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}+(1+\frac{2}{\delta})\beta^{2}\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{2})\left(1-1.84\beta b_{l}+5\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+(1+\frac{2}{\delta})\beta^{2}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~+C_{1}\left(1+\beta b_{u}+\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\right)b_{u}^{-2}|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{2})\left(1-1.84\beta b_{l}+5\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\right)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+(1+\frac{2}{\delta})\beta^{2}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~+C_{1}\left(1+\beta b_{u}+\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\right)\frac{1}{b_{u}^{2}}\left(|\Omega^{*}|+\gamma\alpha d^{*}\right)\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}

(12.29)

where in the second inequality we used

\displaystyle\|{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\|_{\rm F}=\sup_{\|\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\rm F}=1}\langle{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*}),\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\rangle=\sup_{\|\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\rm F}=1}\langle\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{*},{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{Y}})\rangle\leq\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}

(12.30)

since
{\cal P}_{\mathbb{T}_{l-1}}(\bm{\mathcal{Y}})\in\mathbb{M}_{2\mathbf{r}}
and in the last inequality we use
|\Omega^{*}\cup\Omega_{l-1}|\leq|\Omega^{*}|+|\Omega_{l-1}|\leq|\Omega^{*}|+\gamma\alpha d^{*}
.

Now we choose proper
\beta\in[0.005b_{l}/(b_{u}^{2}),0.36b_{l}/(b_{u}^{2})]
so
1-1.84\beta b_{l}+5\beta^{2}b_{u}^{2}\leq 1-\delta
, and we get

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\delta)(1+\delta/2)\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}+3\delta^{-1}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}+C_{1}(b_{u}+1)b_{l}^{-1}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}|+\alpha\gamma d^{*}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

(12.31)

where we use the fact that
\beta\leq 1
.
From the signal-to-noise ratio condition, we have
3\delta^{-1}\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}+C_{1}(b_{u}+1)b_{l}^{-1}\sqrt{|\Omega^{*}|+\alpha\gamma d^{*}}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}\frac{\underline{\lambda}}{C_{m}\sqrt{\bar{r}}}
. This implies that
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{l-1}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}\leq\underline{\lambda}/8
holds.

12.2
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Let
\widehat{\Omega}
and
\Omega^{\ast}
denote the support of
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}
, respectively.
By the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\big|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}\big|\leq\begin{cases}\frac{b_{u}}{b_{l}}\big|[\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}]_{\omega}\big|+\frac{2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}}{b_{l}}&,\textrm{ if }\omega\in\widehat{\Omega}\\
\frac{2b_{u}}{b_{l}}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}+\frac{2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}}{b_{l}}&,\textrm{ if }\omega\in\Omega^{\ast}\setminus\widehat{\Omega}\end{cases}

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\frac{2b_{u}}{b_{l}}\big\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\big\|_{\ell_{\infty}}+\frac{2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}}{b_{l}}.

(12.32)

Now, we can apply Lemma
13.7
and we obtain

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq C_{1,m}\bar{r}^{m/2}\underline{d}^{-(m-1)/2}\mu_{1}^{2m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\rm F}

(12.33)

Now, by putting together (
12.32
), (
12.33
) and (
4.8
), we get

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{S}}}_{l_{\max}}-\bm{\mathcal{S}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq C_{2,m}\kappa_{0}^{2m}\mu_{1}^{2m}\Big(\frac{\bar{r}^{m}}{\underline{d}^{m-1}}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\big(\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}+(|\Omega^{\ast}|+\gamma\alpha d^{\ast})^{1/2}\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\big)+\frac{2\textsf{Err}_{\infty}}{b_{l}},

where
C_{1,m}
and
C_{2,m}
are constants depending only on
m
. Now since we assume
b_{l},b_{u}=O(1)
, we finish the proof of Theorem
4.3
.

12.3
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We first estimate the probability of the following two events.

\displaystyle\textsf{Err}_{2\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0,m}\sigma_{z}\cdot(\bar{d}\bar{r}+r^{\ast})^{1/2}

(12.34)

\displaystyle\textsf{Err}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0,m}^{\prime}\sigma_{z}\log^{1/2}\bar{d}

(12.35)

for some constants
C_{0,m},C_{0,m}^{\prime}>0
depending only on
m
. Notice here the first event (
12.34
) holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{m}\bar{r}\bar{d})
by Lemma
13.3
. And for the second event (
12.35
), we have from the definition,
